===== DOCUMENT PART: WWAP_COA_01-06_Pokegama-NemadjiWetlandsCOA.pdf =====

Extensive deep and shallow marshes border the Pokegama River. Of special significance are the numerous popula�ons of rare plants occurring within the wetlands. Many are represented by large or mul�ple popula�ons throughout the complex and some are not generally widespread within the Lake Superior region. A diversity of animals inhabit the site.
Nemadji River Floodplain Forest, along the banks of the Nemadji River, supports a type of forest unusual in composi�on and perhaps unique in the state. Floodplain forests are very rare along rivers flowing north into Lake Superior. The deep, red clay alluvial soils support an excep�onally rich herb layer, with a species composi�on reminiscent of maplebasswood forests far to the south. 

## Recogni�ons and Designa�ons:

• • Conduct supplemental plan�ngs of coniferous tree species in both wetlands and uplands. • Establish a migratory bird monitoring project ini�a�ve to iden�fy critcal stopover sites.
• Conduct breeding bird surveys every 5 years for two consecu�ve years to determine status and trends of breeding birds and SGCNs.

===== DOCUMENT PART: WWAP_COA_Report_09-11_MuraltBluffCOA.pdf =====

The COA and the Monroe-Muralt Prairie Land Legacy area lie among rolling hills created by an old glacial moraine. The COA includes high quality prairie remnants on the rocky hilltops and slopes that are not �llable, and a small number of prairie pastures and oak savannas. Wet-mesic prairies exist along the valley floors and many rare prairie plants and insects occur in pockets throughout the area.
Although small, Muralt Bluff Prairie and Oliver Prairie SNAs have high-quality dry prairie communi�es that contain numerous rare plants and animals. Abraham's Woods SNA features an old-growth stand of southern mesic forest, which is increasingly rare in Wisconsin. Albany wildlife area has an excellent sand prairie with rich prairie along bike trail. Includes remnants of various stages of oak savanna (Oak Opening through Oak Woodland) and supports rare birds. A ten-mile segment of the Sugar River State Trail has high density of glade mallow.

## Contributors

Over 300 experts and professionals both within and outside the Wisconsin Department of Natural Resources were invited to contribute their knowledge to the content of the Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan. The data, comments and guidance received were critical to revising the Plan.
The development of the 2025-2035 Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan was made possible, by funding from the Wisconsin DNR Bureau of Natural Heritage Conservation.

## Background

In 2001, Congress authorized the U.S. Fish & Wildlife Service to implement and fund a program to help states proactively address the needs of declining wildlife species before they required listing as Endangered or Threatened. The State Wildlife Grants (SWG) Program provides federal grant funds for native wildlife, including species not hunted or fished. Wildlife, as defined by this program, is any species of wild, free-ranging fauna including fish and invertebrates. The SWG Program continues to be the nation's core program for preventing wildlife from becoming endangered.
States must have an approved State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) to receive the federal apportionment or competitive grants through the State Wildlife Grant program
foot_0
 . The Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan (WWAP) is constructed around USFWS's requirement that each state's SWAP address eight elements. How the elements are addressed is largely left up to the needs and circumstances of each state. Use and implementation of the information and conservation actions described in the WWAP are voluntary. Funds must be used to benefit animal species of greatest conservation need (SGCN) and their habitats
foot_1
 as described in each state's Plan. All SWAPs must be revised at intervals not to exceed ten years to continue to receive federal SWG funds.

## The Eight Required Elements for State Wildlife Action Plans

(1) Element 1 Species [Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN)]: Information on the distribution and abundance of species of wildlife, including low and declining populations as the State fish and wildlife agency deems appropriate, that are indicative of the diversity and health of the State's wildlife; and,
(2) Element 2 Habitats and Natural Communities: Descriptions of locations and relative condition of key habitats and community types essential to conservation of species identified in (1); and,
(3) Element 3 Threats: Descriptions of problems which may adversely affect species identified in (1) or their habitats, and priority research and survey efforts needed to identify factors which may assist in restoration and improved conservation of these species and habitats; and, (4) Element 4 Actions: Descriptions of conservation actions proposed to conserve the identified species and habitats and priorities for implementing such actions; and,
(5) Element 5 Monitoring: Proposed plans for monitoring species identified in (1) and their habitats, for monitoring the effectiveness of the conservation actions proposed in (4), and for adapting these conservation actions to respond appropriately to new information or changing conditions; and, (6) Element 6 Review and Revision: Descriptions of procedures to review the strategy at intervals not to exceed ten years; and, (7) Element 7 Partner Involvement: Provisions for coordination during the development, implementation, review, and revision of its Plan with Federal, State, and local agencies and Indian tribes that manage significant land and water areas within the State or administer programs that significantly affect the conservation of identified species and habitats; and, (8) Element 8 Public Involvement: Provisions to provide the necessary public participation in the development, revision and implementation of the Plan.
Currently, states receive funds from the State Wildlife Grants program on an annual basis, which are allocated according to a formula that considers each state's size and population. A portion of grant money is also allocated to competitive grants. Plans are submitted to a regional review team (RRT) comprised of regional representatives from state wildlife agencies and the USFWS, which makes recommendations to the Regional USFWS Director to approve (with or without condition) each revised plan.

## Purpose

This Plan, which has been prepared with input from experts and partners across the state, provides information about species and their habitats to support meaningful conservation decisions regardless of whether conservation is the primary objective or incidental to other objectives.

## Wisconsin's Approach to the WWAP

The WWAP is a comprehensive inventory of the nature and status of rare and declining species, referred to as Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN), natural communities and ecological landscapes within our state.
The WWAP's application goes beyond that of meeting a federal grant requirement. It is a plan for all of Wisconsin to help rare and declining species so that they and the habitats where they live are sustained for future generations. The Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan should not only inform, but be informed by the knowledge, experience and success of its users. Elements of the WWAP are part of a continuous effort of assessment, improvement and implementation.
It is our goal to make this third digital version of the WWAP more useful and accessible to all DNR programs, as well as individuals and organizations outside the DNR that carry out conservation actions in our state regardless of whether they are part of or secondary to their primary objectives. We hope that the WWAP will serve as a living document that both guides conservation priorities for SGCN and their habitat AND is guided by the progress, knowledge gained, and success toward achieving those goals. Together with partners and stakeholders that have a conservation interest, we are looking forward to continuing implementation of the WWAP with better tools to use the plan and monitor the success of our efforts.

## Summary of Outcomes for the 2025 Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan

Although there have been no significant changes to the approach taken to each of the eight elements in the 2025 WWAP, there have been important outcomes and conclusions from this revision.
• The current approaches used to fulfill major elements of the WWAP: NatureServe's conservation status methodology and ranking factors; input from people with knowledge and expertise; association scores to link SGCN, natural communities and landscapes; identification/description of Conservation Opportunity Areas as the best places to carry out conservation; and an organized classification and reporting of priority threats and actions, have persisted as a sound foundation for each iteration of the WWAP.
• Because the overall approach has not significantly changed, the 2025 WWAP and future WWAPs can focus on consistency and tools that take advantage of increasing amounts of data and information to improve accessibility, analysis and outcomes for each element.
• Increasing emphasis on digital or online formats for gathering information (e.g., communitybased monitoring) offers continuous and broad opportunities for input. It also offers access to important data and outcomes that conservation decisionmakers can use to meet their own objectives.
• Plants have been added as SGCN and SGIN to the 2025 WWAP. They have also been assigned association scores, are part of the COA attributes, and Threats and Actions Reports. Although not eligible for State Wildlife Grant funding, their addition fills a critical conservation actions presented in the WWAP will significantly improve conditions for these species, but the reader is reminded these are voluntary options. Both the Threats and Actions classifications follow the Conservation Measures Partnership nomenclature. 
6
 Updated conservation actions and the issues and challenges they address are provided in this WWAP. They are presented by taxonomic group and community group as individual reports in Element 3-4 of the 2025 WWAP collection. This reflects the reality that many of the conservation actions (and threats to conservation) affect multiple species and communities and can be grouped as such.
Development and unsustainable resource use from multiple sectors was commonly cited for terrestrial SGCNs and their habitat, while water use and pollution in the form of fertilizer nutrients and chemicals were commonly cited for aquatic SGCNs and habitat. Actions for these issues were focused on support and compliance with best management practices and conservation planning to manage for diverse habitats to persist on landscapes mixed with these other uses. Improved awareness, training and education for regulatory compliance and to encourage voluntary adoption of standards and practices was frequently cited as essential to meet desired outcomes.

## Summary Element 4 -Conservation Opportunity Areas

Conservation Opportunity Areas (COAs)
foot_6
 are the best places in Wisconsin to implement conservation actions in the Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan (WWAP) to protect and conserve SGCNs and their habitat. They are places on the landscape with significant ecological features, natural communities or SGCN habitats for which Wisconsin is responsible for protecting and conserving. Focusing conservation actions in these prioritized areas can increase the likelihood of long-term success, maximize effectiveness over larger landscapes, improve funding efficiency, and promote voluntary cooperative efforts across ownership boundaries.
Those undertaking conservation actions in our state, whether they are interested individuals, landowners, businesses, conservation groups or resource agencies may focus their actions in conservation opportunity areas. Because of how they are defined, these areas imply greater likelihood of success in helping SGCN and their habitat. COAs are mappable sites relying on readily available georeferenced information and do not identify all the places important or necessary for conservation in our state. Conversely, the boundaries of COAs do not imply that the same level of opportunity for conservation exists throughout them.
The same criteria originally used in the 2005 WWAP have been updated with more current and concise language. Each of the 193 COAs fulfills one or more of these criteria.

Opportunities were noticed through emails, internal newsletter and news release.

## Roadmap and Summary of Revisions to the Eight Elements

The WWAP collection is organized by each of the eight required elements. Each element contains its own collection that consists of a primary text describing the approach, changes from the 2015 WWAP and results. Spreadsheets, reports and other supporting material referenced in the primary text are provided as separate files within that same Element.

• The future of SWAPs lies in our ability to invest in the means to share, organize and access data and information about SGCN, habitats, threats and actions, and to use that investment to facilitate collaboration at all scales --local to regional and beyond.
This submittal of updates to the WWAP is organized into the Elements summarized below.
Once the 2025 WWAP is approved, it will replace content from the 2015 WWAP that is currently available to the public via web-based pages or online text and maps.

## Summary Element 1 -Species of Greatest Conservation Need

The process by which Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) are selected has not changed from the 2015 WWAP, although an effort was made in the current revision to introduce greater consistency and provide easier access to data. Internal and external experts and professionals and internal teams used this process to produce a new list of SGCNs as well as Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), which are plants and animals that either need more information before they can be fully assessed or are borderline SGCN and need to be monitored.
Each major taxonomic group was reassessed. Many species were assessed with the use of NatureServe's Conservation Status Assessment methodology (also known as Natural Heritage Methodology) to derive state and global ranks (SRank and GRank). Ten factors were used to assess conservation status, grouped into three categories: rarity, threats, and trends. In addition to SRanks and GRanks, additional filters were used to place emphasis on characteristics relevant to Wisconsin, including high or moderate vulnerability to climate change, genetically isolated species, cultural significance, regional status, or species with unique habitats in our state. SGIN that could not be assessed were also identified. The same approach was applied to invertebrates, vertebrates and plants 3 . The SGCN selection process ensures a well-documented and consistent assessment of species, which can be readily updated as new information becomes available. Tables 
ES1
 and
ES2
 summarize the results of the SGCN update for the 2025 WWAP and the two previous iterations. The Element 1 folder in the 2025 WWAP collection includes a detailed description of the SGCN selection process and provides SGCN lists, summaries and other related results by taxonomic group.

## Summary Element 2 -SGCN Habitats

For each SGCN, the critical habitats needed to support healthy populations within Wisconsin and the distribution of those habitats in the state were identified in the form of association scores with Natural Communities and Ecological Landscapes (SGCN-NC and SGCN-EL scores rated from 0 = none to 3 = high). Association scores were updated and are summarized below in Tables 
ES3
 and
ES4
. Association scores for each SGCN by taxonomic group are provided in Element 2 of the 2025 WWAP collection. The DNR's Natural Heritage Inventory program 4 was used to identify and define most terrestrial, wetland and aquatic community types used in this analysis. Ecological landscape definitions were taken from the Ecological Landscapes of Wisconsin Report 5 .
All 111 vegetation community types are grouped within eight major habitat groups: anthropogenic, aquatic, barrens and savannas, bedrock and bare soil/sand, prairies and grasslands, northern forest, southern forest and wetland. Each of the 16 Ecological Landscapes were also evaluated to determine which of the natural communities occurring within it present the best opportunities for enhancement and restoration. These natural community and ecological landscape opportunity scores (NC-EL scores) were updated and their results are summarized in Table 
ES5
. All the NC-EL scores are provided in Element 2 of the 2025 WWAP collection for each community type within each of the eight groups.
The three scores (0 to 3) can be used individually or together. A Sum of Scores (Ecological Priorities) tool (0 to 9) was is provided to select and filter for combinations of SGCN, natural community and ecological landscape. Locations where the sum of scores is > 6 indicates an ecological priority. 

## Summary Elements 3 and 4 -Threats and Conservation Actions for SGCN and their Habitat

Issues and challenges that affect conservation of SGCN and natural communities (habitat) and the conservation actions they address, were updated by the Taxon Teams and partners. Many issues and conservation actions operate on a habitat or natural community level and may be coordinated to simultaneously address the needs of multiple species. Implementing the 

## Summary Element 5 -Monitoring

Monitoring focuses on opportunities to measure the implementation of priority conservation actions. Given the breadth and complexity of the WWAP elements and the diversity of partners and WWAP users who implement conservation actions, it is not feasible to effect comprehensive WWAP monitoring. Rather Element 5 as it relates to SGCN, habitats, threats and actions, is currently fulfilled by NHC and the Department through multiple types of monitoring:
• Wisconsin Forest/Field Inventory and Reporting System (WisFIRS) allows foresters, biologists, and ecologists to store data collected in the field, plan for and track completed treatments.
• Annual Property Implementation Plans (APIP) and Plan Monitoring Reports share with the public and partners the major scheduled and completed forest and habitat management treatments, recreation and infrastructure development projects and other property management actions. • The Natural Heritage Inventory program applies a standard methodology for collecting, characterizing and managing data, making it possible to combine data at various scales to address local, state, regional and national issues. • Grant and Other Project Reporting such as the Wildlife Tracking and Reporting Actions for the Conservation of Species (TRACS), a reporting system used by the U.S. Fish & Wildlife Service's (USFWS) Office of Conservation Investment (OIC) that captures information about conservation and related actions funded by its grant programs. • Surface Water Integrated Monitoring System (SWIMS) provides science-based monitoring, analysis and regulation of discharges to surface waters to protect and maintain the water quality in Wisconsin through management actions including aquatic plant management, aquatic invasive species initiatives, and lakes, rivers, and wetland grants.

## Summary Element 6 -Plan Review and Revision

The SGCN selection process and effectiveness monitoring measures, as well as database support tools, are all intended to facilitate a timely and consistent evaluation of the status and response of SGCN and their habitats to conservation actions. Element 6 describes the tools and schedule that form the basis of a well-documented response to changing environmental conditions and changes in the status of SGCN and their habitat.

## Summary Elements 7 and 8 Partner and Public Involvement

Elements 7 and 8 describe how conservation partners and the public have been involved in its development as well as plans for continued participation during plan implementation. A large group of experts representing a broad range of conservation interests from the state were invited to provide input for SGCN taxonomic groups, natural community groups, threats and actions. This includes state and federal agencies, private wildlife conservation organizations, the academic community, Tribal Nations and other programs within the Department. The Methods sec�on also describes external expert input for iden�fying SGCN that is also part of Element 8 Public Par�cipa�on.
Species of Greatest Conserva�on Need (SGCN) have low and declining popula�ons and are in need of conserva�on ac�on. In the 2025 WWAP, SGCN include invertebrates, like insects, crayfish, snails and mussels, as well as vascular plants, lichens, mosses, birds, fish, mammals, rep�les and amphibians that are:
• federal or state listed as threatened or endangered;
• experiencing threats to their life history needs or habitats;
• few or low in abundance or distribu�on; or • currently not rare but showing declines in abundance, distribu�on or habitat.
This Plan also dis�nguishes Species of Greatest Informa�on Need (SGIN) of two types:
• SGIN -Monitoring. This includes species that did not meet the criteria of SGCN (i.e., borderline or almost SGCN) that need to be monitored as poten�al SGCN. • SGIN -Ranking. Species with uncertain status. Informa�on is needed to rank the species or add certainty to the exis�ng rank. This category includes species or en�re taxonomic groups. The later is most relevant to invertebrates and plants. These species may be reclassified as SGCN.

## Identify Expert Lists and Initiate Team

Each species group is represented by a Taxon Team made up of staff from the DNR's Bureau of Natural Heritage Conserva�on (NHC): aqua�c invertebrates/fish, birds, herp�les, mammals, plants and natural communi�es, and terrestrial invertebrates. The Teams are a permanent part of NHC's organiza�onal structure. Each Team compiled a list of experts
foot_0
 with knowledge about the species and the factors used to assess them. The list was/is flexible in that people were added or removed based on their expressed interest, availability or the recommenda�ons of others. Notwithstanding addi�ons or dele�ons over �me, this list was used for review of Associa�on Scores (Element 2) and Threats and Ac�ons (Elements 3 and 4). To improve consistency and facilitate discussion and decision-making many resources were created for the Taxon Teams. This included the following five guides:
• SGCN/SGIN Flowchart to help organize and prompt decisions about species that would be SGCN, SGIN or neither, once S-Ranks were updated or confirmed. Appendix E1, Item 1. • S3-S3/S4 Guidance for assessing "in-between" species that were not clearly SGCN (i.e., S3, S3S4 species). Appendix E1, Item 2. • SGIN Guidance for the subset of species that are not SGCN that s�ll have significant monitoring or informa�on need. Appendix E1, Item 3. • Rank Calculator Guide captures the most important aspects of the rank calculator and prompts ques�ons and decisions

 . • Ranking and SGCN/SGIN Resources were presented to the Taxon Teams at a training session prior to beginning this task.

## Identify Species to Be Assessed

Each Taxon Team considered Wisconsin's na�ve species and whether and to what degree to assess them. 
6
 Teams determined which species needed to be ranked or have their ranks assessed, and which of those species would be run through the rank calculator based largely on whether there was new informa�on since the 2015 WWAP. This exercise yielded three approximate groups: Species readily iden�fied as common or secure: This group includes Wisconsin's most common species like grey squirrel and American robin and species ranked as secure (S5) or apparently secure (S4). The Bird Taxon Team, for example, did not assess the most common, widespread and abundant species with no new conserva�on concerns or significant declines.
Species assessed using the NatureServe rank calculator: This group of species was assessed using NatureServe's rank calculator program to facilitate the process of determining conserva�on status ranks. Generally, these were species where there was sufficient new informa�on to run or rerun the calculator with confidence and may include:
• Species with an imperiled rank or a non-imperiled rank that are State Endangered or Threatened.
• Uncommon species with new data on status, trends or threats since 2014: species with community-based monitoring (CBM) programs 7 ; species with targeted surveys completed through grants and other funding sources; 2015 WWAP SGCN and SGIN; and new or updated G-rank; R-SGCN results.

## Defini�ons of S-Ranks

• SU -Unrankable due to lack of informa�on or substan�ally conflic�ng informa�on about status or trends. • SH -Known from only historical records. The element may no longer be present in Wisconsin, but there is insufficient evidence to state this with certainty. The SH rank is used when an element's presence has not been documented in decades despite some searching, and there is evidence of significant habitat loss or degrada�on, or when an element has been searched unsuccessfully but not thoroughly enough to presume that it is no longer present in Wisconsin. • SX -Presumed to be ex�rpated from Wisconsin and not located despite intensive searches of historical sites and another appropriate habitat, and virtually no likelihood that it will be rediscovered.
The S/G Rank Method depends on the State(S)-and Global(G)-ranks defined by the NatureServe Conserva�on Status Assessment methodology (also called NatureServe Core Methodology) and the accompanying rank calculator program, V3.1932 (or V3.2). Ten factors are used to assess conserva�on status, grouped into three categories: rarity, threats, and trends (Appendix 1 Item 6 Rank Calculator Factor Reference). Teams and experts reviewed the rank factors that are part of NatureServe's Conserva�on Status Assessment to determine if there was addi�onal informa�on to change them or improve their accuracy. Teams es�mate each factor and enter them into the rank calculator. An example of rank calculator results for digger crayfish is provided in Appendix E1, Item 7, Digger Crayfish Example.
The calculator works in combina�on with NatureServe's web-enabled system for biodiversity informa�on management (Bio�cs 5) 
10
 . The NHC Biodiversity Database (Appendix E1, Item 8, Example from NHC Biodiversity Database) was created in Microso� Access to organize species informa�on used by the Taxon Teams to assess species rarity and to capture public input, updated rarity S-Ranks, proposed 2025 WWAP status, community and landscape associa�on scores for proposed SGCNs (Element 2) and a summary of current state of knowledge about the species in Wisconsin.
Over 4000 plant and animal species are in the database, including 200 species proposed for addi�on to the state's list of species via public and staff input.
The process s�ll involves interpreta�on and opinion, but the rank calculator compels an orderly and replicable process that is na�onally and interna�onally recognized, to make transparent decisions regarding the status and vulnerability of species and ensures con�nuity for subsequent revisions. Invertebrate and Plant species did however, present their own circumstances described further below.
Results from Biodiversity Database can be readily reported for review and analysis. For each species where new informa�on was available or where there were ques�ons about the accuracy of the exis�ng rank, the available data were incorporated into the rank calculator.
Approximately 951 S-Ranks were updated.

## Ranking of Aqua�c and Terrestrial

Invertebrates: These species groups present challenges because of the sheer number of taxonomic groups and species. Teams discussed whether there was reasonably available informa�on that could be used to improve ranks. Species that could not be reviewed because of lack of exper�se, survey effort or ques�ons about taxonomy were flagged as poten�al SGIN -Ranking. Many invertebrate groups, for example, lack sufficient survey effort to run the rank calculator with confidence. Instead, Taxon Teams used knowledge of life history and habitat associa�ons to assess popula�on condi�on. Aqua�c and terrestrial invert Taxon Teams started their assessment with groups that had the most observa�on data that could be run through the rank calculator (e.g., bumble bees, mussels, dragonflies), typically supported by Community Based Monitoring efforts. The Teams then proceeded by taxonomic order and assessed species groups with NHC staff exper�se as well as other DNR staff and external experts to fill knowledge gaps.

## Ranking of Vascular Plants, Non-vascular Plants (Mosses) and Lichens (referred to collec�vely as Plants):

The Plants and Natural Communi�es Team reviewed ranks for vascular plants, nonvascular plants and lichens. Each of these groups was assessed using the NatureServe Rank Calculator and data from the Wisconsin Natural Heritage Inventory Database as well as herbarium specimens, iNaturalist observa�ons, and expert input. Because there is significantly less exper�se and data available for non-vascular plants and lichens, considera�on of habitat specializa�on and threats was weighed more heavily than short-and long-term trends and even popula�on size since informa�on on the number of individuals was rarely reported. Also, for these groups applica�on of the state historic (SH) rank was different than for vascular plants.
Typically, vascular plants that have not been reported in 40 years or more are given an SH rank and put on the SGIN list. Since there are many �mes fewer collec�ons and reports of nonvascular plants and lichens, the team decided to apply a limit of 100 years before ranking a species as SH.

## Derive Proposed SGCN and SGIN Lists

Once S-Ranks were updated, Taxon Teams used internally developed aids: the SGCN/SGIN Flowchart, S3-S3/S4 Guidance and SGIN Guidance, (Appendix E1, Items 1 to 3) to help them assess whether species were SGCN, SGIN or neither. This process was itera�ve, par�cularly since knowledge submited by experts during review of the proposed lists mo�vated reevalua�on of a species by the Taxon Team.

## SGCN List

Not all na�ve Wisconsin species are on the SGCN list. S-Ranks help to iden�fy those that are common and rela�vely stable and therefore, not of greatest conserva�on need. Others may be in apparent need of conserva�on, but litle is currently known about their status. Using the SGCN/SGIN Flowchart as a guide (appendix E1 -Item 1), Taxon Teams iden�fied species that were obviously SGCN based on their ranks (e.g., S1 or S2). They then went on to evaluate species that were in the middle (e.g., S3, S3S4 and S4) following the S3-S3/S4 Guidance.

## SGIN List

These are species that do not have enough informa�on to carry them through the SGCN decision process or are on the borderline for qualifying as an SGCN. To help focus research and inventory needs, they are divided into two categories:
SGIN -Monitoring: These are borderline SGCN that might need to be monitored. 
11
 The designa�on is applicable to species that were removed from the SGCN list or may be added to the list in the future. This may include species that are uncommon (not rare), S-Rank = S3?, S3, S3S4, or S4? and where one or more of the following applies: • shows declining regional, range-wide or global trend data; anecdotal or local reports of declining trends; • may s�ll be stable in the state or slightly declining but s�ll have a robust popula�on (i.e., statewide data may not reflect the trends noted above); • condi�on or status may be subject to disagreement among collaborators;
• is par�cularly vulnerable to an emerging threat or combina�on of threats; or • is designated as Regional-SGCN, regional Species of Concern (SOC), species with tribal significance, etc.
SGIN -Ranking: These are species with uncertain status. Informa�on is needed to rank species with uncertain or weak S-Ranks. The goal is to be able to rank the species. This may include species with S-Rank = SNR 12 , SU, SH, S2S4, S3? and where one or more of the following circumstances applies:
• possibly ex�rpated = Historic (SH)
foot_7
 ; species has not been reported in decades and evidence of habitat loss and has been searched for but not thoroughly. Surveys are needed in suitable habitat within its historic range in WI. • reviewed and unrankable (SU), have a range rank (S2S4), an inexact rank (S3?), cannot be confidently ranked because data are lacking on range, occupancy, trends or threats.
For invertebrates and plans this includes individual species when the data need/approach is species-specific; and species assemblages when the need/approach is broad (e.g., baseline surveys/data to determine range and occupancy for aqua�c insects or prairie lea�oppers). • if a species is ranked S2S4 due to uncertainty about a threat, where it is infeasible to gather enough information about the threat to reduce the range in ranking, this species may be evaluated along with S3 and S3S4 species for SGCN status.

## Neither SGCN or SGIN -Rela�vely Stable or Common

In this group remain all the other species that are not SGCN or SGIN. This includes species that had sufficient informa�on to assess them with confidence and did not meet the SGCN criteria (e.g., ranked S4 or S5, ranked S3G5 or S3S4G5, or did not meet the criteria used in addi�on to S/G-Ranks). This also includes common species that were not assessed. Species that are currently stable or common may be affected by environmental changes due to disease, large-or broad-scale trends in land or resources use, climate change, etc. Therefore, like all na�ve species in the state, they will con�nue to have their status evaluated. The SGCN assessment process is fluid, reflec�ng the reality of the threats and conserva�on ac�ons affec�ng 12 If a species has been reviewed and added to the SGIN -Monitoring category, its SNR rank should automatically be changed to SU. The database manager will make sure this happens. SNA species are not considered.

## E1.3 Summary of Changes to Element 1 from the 2015 WWAP

There were no significant changes in the basic approach to iden�fying SGCN and SGIN or the methodology that was used.
The 2025 WWAP relied on the same NatureServe Conserva�on Status Assessment Methodology and accompanying Rank Calculator for ranking and iden�fying SGCN and SGIN as was used for the 2015 WWAP. Once ranks were derived, there was further assessment and discussion considering the same factors as those used in the 2015 WWAP. Species that did not have sufficient informa�on or that did not meet the criteria for SGCN but were of concern to the experts were added to the Species Greatest Informa�on Need (SGIN) list. Exper�se, knowledge and data sources were used.
Notable but less significant changes to the approach taken for Element 1 in the 2025 WWAP versus the 2015 WWAP are summarized below.
• Version 3.1932 (1/10/2020) of the rank calculator was used. • Flowcharts and guidelines (Appendix E1, Items 1 to 3) provided improved instruc�on and decision-making rigor to reduce inconsistency among the Taxon Teams (e.g., exclusion of historical data). • We relied on email, online fillable forms and personal follow-up with experts as needed. Inperson mee�ngs with experts did not take place. We felt this approach was a more cost-effec�ve way to reach more people to get feedback. • The name and defini�on of SGIN was narrowed from three to two categories. Species with Informa�on Needs (SIN) became Species of Greatest Informa�on Need (SGIN). Basic and Ranking SGIN were combined into Ranking SGIN to reflect the overall need for popula�on and habitat informa�on for these species or groups. • Generally, the factors considered in the rank calculator and subsequent evalua�ons in the 2015 WWAP were considered in the 2025 WWAP, but Vulnerability and Wisconsin Responsibility factors were more clearly described in guidance to the Taxon Teams. • Regional SGCN, which were not available for the 2015 WWAP, were included among the factors in post-ranking evalua�ons. • There were more data for most taxonomic groups; much of which came from communitybased monitoring (CBM) programs. • The 2015 WWAP considered a breeding and non-breeding state rank (e.g., B3N3). The nonbreeding rank has been removed. State ranks were assessed for bird species that nested in Wisconsin in at least three of the last five years. To simplify and standardize the ranking process, the resul�ng ranks are thus for nes�ng species and, importantly, reflect only the species' breeding status.

## E1.4 Challenges Assessing Species Ranks

Although each Taxon Team followed the same guidelines for iden�fying SGCN/SGIN, there are factors or challenges that may have weighed differently on a Team's decisions for the species within that taxon.
• The propor�on of species that were assigned S-Ranks using the rank assessment calculator varied. The rank calculator was used for many species in the herp�le, bird and mammal groups. For the larger taxonomic groups like plants and invertebrates, propor�onally fewer species were assessed using this tool because each "run" of the calculator is �me consuming, par�cularly if discussion is needed to supplement limited or inconclusive data. • The alterna�ve approach was to assign ranks through team discussion of the ranking factors (rarity, threats and trends). Species that were obviously at risk (ranked S1 or S2) or those that were obviously common or stable (S4 or S5) generally were not run through the calculator.
• Taxon Teams differed in the informa�on available to assign values to the factors used to assess "threats" and "trends" categories. In many cases factors in these categories were broadly es�mated based on the group's conclusions. • Some species may have a State Rank of S1, S2, S3, where the primary or only ac�on to consider is research about taxonomy, popula�on size or distribu�on, past trends, life history, ecology, issues or conserva�on ac�ons. While there may be enough informa�on to assign a rank, there is not enough informa�on to know what conserva�on ac�ons will help them. • Experts and professionals approach this type of assessment differently and so it is not possible to reach a consensus or near consensus for all species. For example, some assessors may be reluctant to make es�mates despite the accepted uncertainty inherent in the ranking assessment. • The rank calculator weighs many factors that reflect the complexity of assessing species' status. Teams may decide the outcome for one of the three categories does not adequately reflect the circumstances of a species. The methodology allows for inclusion of condi�onal factors if informa�on is limited (e.g. intrinsic vulnerability to threats) and adjustments for ecological considera�ons or specific life history traits. Allowing space for knowledge and experience is cri�cal but �me intensive. • Changes in a species distribu�on and popula�on size are considered statewide. Local ex�rpa�ons might not impact the rank when other popula�ons are increasing or stable. And yet, local ex�rpa�ons and changes may be very important indicators of risk for a species. In these cases, designa�ng a species as SGIN-monitoring and/or local assessment of status and conserva�on needs are needed.

## Mammal SGCN, SGIN, and other species not SGCN

Mammals play an important role in ecosystem function as top of the food-chain predators, and likewise, serving as prey for numerous other predators. Mammals provide an important means of disturbance for plant communities and by indirect influence, the size and composition of many insect communities, as well. There are 71 native mammal species in Wisconsin. Eighteen were reviewed and assessed by the Mammal Taxon Team using the rank calculator and 53 without, and 11 (15%) were identified as SGCN and 10 (14%) were identified as SGIN (Table 
E1
.3). Six are listed as Threatened or Endangered at the state or federal level.
Information about the status, distribution and biology of mammal SGCN can be found on the NHI biodiversity pages. 
16
 A comprehensive table with SGCN/SGIN results for all animal and plant groups, including summarized notes, can be found in the 2025 WWAP collection for Element 1.
foot_11
 A summary of changes between the 2015 and 2025 WWAP for all species is provided in Table 
E1
.13 at the end of this section. In addition to the NHI database and expert knowledge, a wide variety of resources were used during the assessment process, including information from the Midwest Bat Working Group (MWBWG) and recent small mammal monitoring efforts. These and other data informed the process to help determine which species should be or remain SGCN, SGIN, or removed from the list altogether. Some species like American Water Shrew (Sorex palustris) were moved from SGCN to SGIN due to vulnerability in Wisconsin and the need for more survey effort. Others, such as Little Brown Bat (Myotis lucifigus), remained SGCN due to the many threats and large population declines.
Although all taxonomic groups used the same process to identify Wisconsin's SGCN, bat species face an uncertain future from the long-term threat of White-nose Syndrome (WNS In relation to assessing small mammals for SGCN, a 2014 publication by Stephens and Anderson shed much light on the habitat associations and assemblages of small mammals throughout the state in numerous plant communities. These data were tremendously helpful in assessing the status of many small mammals and reinforced the rarity of many of those found on the initial SGCN list (WDNR 2005) that remain on the list in 2025. There is still a paucity of information for several of our small mammal species due to their cryptic nature, lack of effective and reliable detection methods, and/or simply low population numbers.
Of the state's remaining native mammal species, some are very common or stable and relatively speaking are not in need of conservation (e.g., raccoon, striped skunk) or they may be managed as game species (e.g., deer). These mammals were not assessed for SGCN status. For some mammal species an assessment was deemed "not applicable" (NatureServe Rank = SNA) because the species is not a suitable target for conservation activities. A species can be "not applicable" for several reasons: their presence here is unpredictable or infrequent, there are no permanent breeding pairs, there isn't a sustainable population, or we can't identify a habitat or territory that it uses. These species include cougar, Canada lynx and Indiana bat. In our state, these species bring important issues to the forefront about what defines a native species and the circumstances that qualify a species for conservation need, especially with species and habitat shifts occurring because of climate and other environmental changes.

## References for Mammal Species of Greatest Conservation Need

Information sources used in the evaluation and assessment of mammal species SGCN/SGIN are described in Appendix E1, Item 4 Species Information Sources. Some information about rare species locations is confidential or comes to us through informal technical reports or memos.
For these various reasons, we also relied significantly on expert and professional observations and unpublished data. Species experts assessed each species' rarity, threats and trends according to the NatureServe Conservation Status Assessment Methodology. Vulnerability to environmental change, status in Wisconsin and genetic diversity were also assessed. Species were then each assigned an Srank
foot_13
 following NatureServe's Natural Heritage Methodology and those with an S-rank of S1 or S2, and some species with an S-rank of S3 or any combination thereof (e.g., S1S2, S2S3) became SGCN. Species that do not have sufficient information to be ranked or do not meet the criteria for SGCN but are of concern to experts become Species of Information Need (SGIN): SGINranking or SGIN-monitoring, respectively. Other species had sufficient information to assess their SGCN status, and did not meet the SGCN criteria; however, information may still be gathered to monitor their populations and habitat in the event their status changes. Details on the process of identifying SGCN and SGIN can be found above in E1.2 Methods.
Beginning in 2023, state ranks were assessed for bird species that nested in Wisconsin in at least three of the last five years; the resulting ranks reflect only Wisconsin's nesting species, and importantly, on the species' breeding status in the state. Although we acknowledge the migratory nature of many birds, non-breeding status
foot_14
 is no longer used to simplify and standardize the ranking process, although the full annual life cycle was considered during the rank assessment process. Efforts to address species' conservation needs during migration and winter remain important. For some non-breeding species, their habitat and conservation requirements can be met through ranked breeding birds, while others, like shorebirds, are incorporated by proxy through the plan's threats and actions that are outside the context of ranking or SGCN/SGIN designation.

## Bird SGCN, SGIN, and other species not SGCN

Birds play an important role in ecosystem function and a wide array of roles in the habitats in which they occur. Wisconsin provides important breeding, wintering, or migratory habitat for approximately 284 native bird species, which includes 225 breeding birds and another 50+ migrant/wintering species. 
21
 Of the 284 species, 165 were reviewed and assessed by the Bird Taxon Team without the calculator and 65 with the rank calculator. From this, 49 (17%) of native bird species were identified as SGCN and 22 (8%) were identified as SGIN (Table 
E1
.4). Twenty-two are currently listed as Threatened or Endangered in Wisconsin; one is listed as federally Threatened or Endangered, and one is listed federally as an Experimental, Non-Essential Population.
Information about the status, distribution and biology of bird SGCNs can be found on the NHI biodiversity pages. 
22
 A comprehensive table with SGCN/SGIN results for all animal and plant groups, including summarized notes, can be found in the 2025 WWAP collection for Element 1.
foot_17
 A summary of changes between the 2015 and 2025 WWAP for all species is provided in Table 
E1
.13 at the end of this section.
The DNR's Breeding Bird Atlas II
foot_18
 , which ran from 2015-2019, helped document the distribution and abundance of breeding birds in the state and significantly improved our understanding of their population changes in light of the numerous threats they face, including climate change and loss of habitat. Along with other resources, the atlas helped inform the assessment process in determining which species should be or remain SGCN, SGIN, or removed from the list altogether. Some species like American Woodcock were removed from the SGCN list due to a population increase. Others, such as Dark-eyed Junco, were added as SGIN due to declining trends and vulnerability to climate change and to highlight future monitoring needs.
Of our state's remaining native bird species, some are very common or stable and, relatively speaking, are not in need of conservation (e.g., blue jay and American crow) and some of those may be managed as game species (e.g., mourning dove and wild turkey). For some bird species an assessment was considered not applicable (rank = SNA) because the species is not a suitable target for conservation activities. A species can be "not applicable" for a couple reasons: their presence here is unpredictable or infrequent, there are no permanent breeding pairs, there isn't a sustainable population, or we can't identify a habitat or territory that it uses. These species include long distance migrants such as the marbled godwit, and species that do not regularly breed within the state such as the barn owl and snowy egret. In our state, these species bring important issues to the forefront about what defines a native species and the circumstances that qualify a species for conservation need, especially with species and habitat shifts occurring as a result of climate and other environmental changes.

## Fish SGCN, SGIN, and other species not SGCN

There are 148 native fish species in Wisconsin. Of these, 40 were reviewed and 23 (16%) were identified as SSGCN and 7 (18%) were identified as SGIN in Wisconsin. Twenty are listed as Threatened or Endangered in Wisconsin and two are Special Concern. Fish SGCN and SGIN are listed in Table 
E1
.5.
Information about the status, distribution and biology of fish SGCN can be found on the NHI biodiversity pages. 
27
 A comprehensive table with SGCN/SGIN results for all animal and plant groups, including summarized notes, can be found in the 2025 WWAP collection for Element 1. 
28
 Many of our fish Species of Greatest Conservation Need are found only in large river systems such as the Mississippi and Wisconsin rivers, which have been highly altered with dams and other hydraulic control infrastructure. Restoration of the natural processes that characterize these systems would help conserve many of these species by providing the natural flow regime, connectivity and specific habitats that these large river species need throughout their life cycles. Most fish SGCN are warm water habitat species that coincide with areas of the state where development and use of aquatic resources and adjacent lands is greatest, which underscores the need and opportunity for collaborative conservation.
Of our states remaining, non-SGCN or SGIN native fish species, some are common or stable and presently are not in need of conservation (e.g., rainbow darter, southern redbelly dace, tadpole madtom) or they are managed as species that are fished (e.g., brook trout, walleye, smallmouth bass). These fish species were not assessed for SGCN status. Presently there are no fish species ranked as SNA or "not applicable" because they are an unsuitable target for conservation activities or their presence here is unpredictable or infrequent. However, changes and extreme fluctuations in water temperature, quality and quantity as well as competition for surface and ground water resources may result in changes abundance and distribution (i.e., the status of a species).

## Herptile SGCN, SGIN, and other species not SGIN

Herptiles use a wide variety of habitats from sand prairies to streams to ephemeral ponds. Many herptile SGCN are distinguished by their use of a combination of terrestrial, wetland and aquatic habitats to meet their life history and ecological needs. As a group, this makes them vulnerable to threats from sources acting in multiple environments. There are 55 native herptile 32 species in Wisconsin, which includes 36 reptiles and 19 amphibians. In all, 38 species were reviewed and assessed without the calculator and 20 with the rank calculator. This included 3 historical species no longer considered present. As a result, 25 (45%) were identified as SGCN and four as SGIN (Table 
1
.4). Eight are currently listed as Threatened or Endangered in Wisconsin.
Information about the status, distribution and biology of amphibian and reptile SGCN can be found on the NHI biodiversity pages. 
33
 animal and plant groups, including summarized notes, can be found in the 2025 WWAP collection for Element 1.
foot_25
In addition to the NHI database and expert knowledge, a wide variety of resources were used during the assessment process, including the Wisconsin Frog and Toad Survey a citizen-based monitoring program that began in 1981 to assess status, distribution and long-term population trends of Wisconsin's twelve frog and toad species. These and other data helped herp experts determine which species should be or remain SGCN, SGIN, or removed from the list altogether. Some species like the Common Five-lined Skink (Plestiodon fasciatus) are widespread but were added as SGIN to highlight the need for more information in light of the many threats it currently faces. Whereas the northern leopard frog (Lithobates pipiens) was removed as SGIN as the species is apparently now secure and no longer in need of close monitoring.

## Invertebrate Animal SGCN, SGIN, and other species not SGCN

Invertebrate animals make up 94% of the world's animal species. There are estimated to be over 25,000 native invertebrate species in Wisconsin. Of these 1048 (4%) were assessed and 335 (1.3%) have been identified as SGCN and SGIN in Wisconsin (Table 
E1
.9). Forty-four are currently listed as Threatened or Endangered in Wisconsin. Nine are federally endangered, three are federal Species of Concern, two are proposed for federal listing and one is a candidate for listing.
Information about the status, distribution and biology of invertebrate SGCN can be found on the NHI biodiversity pages. 
38
 The Wisconsin Bumble Bee Brigade (DNR), Wisconsin Odonata Survey (DNR and Wisconsin Dragonfly Society) and Wisconsin Mussel Monitoring (DNR) projects, along with crowd-sourced observation platforms such as iNaturalist and BugGuide, helped document the distribution and abundance of many invertebrate groups in the state and significantly improved our understanding of their population changes in light of the numerous threats they face, including climate change and loss of habitat. Along with other resources and via expert input, these monitoring projects provided critical data needed to determine which species should be SGCN, SGIN, or removed from the list altogether. Some species like Yellow Bumble Bee were removed from the SGCN list due to its wide range and occupancy in the state. Others, such as Northern Clearwater Crayfish, were added as SGIN due to declining trends regionally and vulnerability to climate change and to highlight future monitoring needs.
Other groups such as grasshoppers, beetles, snails, spiders, crustaceans, mayflies, stoneflies, specialist bees, caddisflies and true bugs lack statewide survey effort and do not have aggregated datasets needed to quantify range and abundance in the state. Instead, we relied heavily upon input by experts to identify species with narrow habitat specificity and species sensitive to degraded habitat quality. Many 2015 SGCNs from these groups were added to the 2025 SGIN list when research and inventory were identified as the primary conservation need. Additionally, there are many groups for which we cannot even compile a Wisconsin species list much less describe which species are of conservation need. The lack of information has fostered extensive public misunderstanding regarding many invertebrate species. Wisconsin is not alone; most invertebrate groups have not been studied or catalogued and basic lists of species are lacking for most taxa in most states.
Of our state's remaining native invertebrate species, some are very common or stable and, relatively speaking, are not in need of conservation (e.g., Common Green Darner dragonfly and Painted Lady butterfly). For some invertebrate species an assessment was considered not applicable (rank = SNA) because the species is not a suitable target for conservation activities. A species can be "not applicable" for a couple reasons: their presence here is unpredictable or infrequent, their presence has not been confirmed and they are not considered native. These species include facultative migrants such as the Painted Skimmer and Comet Darner dragonflies, and species that do not regularly occur within the state such as the Southern Cloudy Wing butterfly. In our state, these species bring important issues to the forefront about what defines a native species and the circumstances that qualify a species for conservation need, especially with species and habitat shifts occurring as a result of climate and other environmental changes.

## SGCN, SGIN, and other species not SGCN

The Wisconsin WAP stresses the importance of protecting habitats as a means of protecting entire suites of species, which is why plant SGCNs have been incorporated into this plan. The first plan (2005) stated that protecting "rare plants" was a management opportunity for some natural communities or ecological landscapes. However, by specifically identifying and describing plant SGCNs in the same way as animal SGCNs (i.e., with association scores, threats and actions, and Conservation Opportunity Areas), users can make more complete and comprehensive decisions about conservation actions, their targets and where to implement them on the landscape, giving the plan greater value as a centralized, go-to source for conservation priorities and options that incorporate complete ecosystems.
There are roughly 2,640 45 native vascular plants (including hybrids and infraspecific taxa) in Wisconsin, which is similar to other states in the Upper Great Lakes region. Of these, 318 vascular plant species were reviewed and assessed by the Plant Taxon Team without the rank calculator and 69 with the calculator. Another 194 bryophytes and lichens were also reviewed without the rank calculator. Of the native vascular species, 295 (11%) were identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need and 26 as SGIN (Table 
E1
.10). A total of 130 native vascular plants are listed as state threatened or endangered, 192 as special concern and six plants are listed as federally threatened. For bryophytes, 105 were reviewed with 59 identified as SGCN and 21 as SGIN. Similarly, 105 lichens were reviewed with 85 identified as SGCN and 9 as SGIN.
Information about the status, distribution and biology of vascular plant (i.e., mosses and lichens are not included) SGCN can be found on the NHI biodiversity pages. 
46
 A comprehensive table with SGCN/SGIN results for all animal and plant groups, including summarized notes, can be found in the 2025 WWAP collection for Element 1.
foot_34
 A summary of changes between the 2015 and 2025 WWAP for all species is provided in Table 
E1
.11 at the end of this section.
Rare plant habitats can vary greatly in size and quality, with species occurring in both large blocks of high-quality undisturbed habitats as well as small, remnant, isolated sites. The plant team recognized that unknown trends or uncertain threat impacts to plants were a common challenge to defining SGCNs.
Of our states remaining native plant species, some are common or stable and relatively speaking are not in need of conservation (e.g., sugar maple, sweet cicely, blue-joint grass, etc.). These plant species were not assessed for SGCN status. Environmental changes and the fact that many rare species persist as unknown remnant populations, will necessitate periodic reassessment of how plant species are distributed among these categories (i.e., common/stable, assessed but not SGCN or SGIN).

While others may have had a greater range and abundance and have experienced or are experiencing a decline. • Decision 5: Rare or uncommon Globally with G-Rank = G1-G4 or weak/uncertain G-Rank? Species that are rare or uncommon globally and at moderate risk in our state (S3) or that range from moderate risk to apparently secure (S3S4) and are globally secure (G-Rank = 5) should be reviewed for SGCN considera�on as Wisconsin may be responsible for a significant por�on of the species range.

[FIGURE: 9 • S1 -Cri�cally imperiled in Wisconsin due to a restricted range, few popula�ons or occurrences, steep declines, severe threats, or other factors. • S2 -Imperiled in Wisconsin due to a restricted range, few popula�ons or occurrences, steep declines, severe threats, or other factors. • S3 -Vulnerable in Wisconsin due to a somewhat restricted range, rela�vely few popula�ons or occurrences, recent and widespread declines, threats, or other factors. • S4 -Apparently secure in Wisconsin due to an extensive range and/or many popula�ons or occurrences, but with possible cause for some concern due to recent local declines, threats, or other factors. • S5 -Secure in Wisconsin due to a very extensive range, abundant popula�ons or occurrences, with litle to no concern from declines or threats. • S#S# -A range rank (S2S3, S1S3) indicates any uncertainty regarding the element's status in Wisconsin.]

[FIGURE: Results -Amphibian and Rep�le Species of Greatest Conserva�on NeedHerptile (amphibians and reptiles) Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) have low and declining populations and need conservation action. In general, species that are state Threatened or Endangered, experiencing life history or habitat threats, are few or in low abundance/distribution, or showing declines in abundance or habitat become SGCN. They have been defined based in part on state and global ranks developed through Wisconsin's Natural Heritage Inventory (NHI) program and other criteria that address species or taxonomic characteristics not captured by the ranking method alone. As part of the 2015 Wildlife Action Plan, a formal, reproducible process to identify SGCN was established and again used in 2025.]

[FIGURE: Con�nued, Appendix E1.1 SGCN and SGIN Selec�on Flowchart -Ra�onale The flowcharts assume that state conserva�on ranks (S-Ranks) have been updated and global ranks (G-Ranks) have been reviewed to flag ones that are dated, incomplete or faulty. The flowchart should only be used for species that are considered na�ve, regular breeders in Wisconsin. • Decision 1: State listed THR, END or has imperiled S-Rank (S1, S2, S1S2)? The objec�ve of the SWAPs and the State Wildlife Grant program is to help species before they become listed as THR or END. However, the scope of the WWAP and its poten�al user base create an inevitable need to NOT exclude listed species because it may make the WWAP incompa�ble with other regulatory responsibili�es, incorrectly imply these are low priority, and/or create a disconnect with the mission of poten�al WWAP users. • Decision 2: Federally listed THR, END and has a numeric breeding S-Rank? [TABLE: 9 Results -Invertebrate Species of Greatest Conserva�on Need Invertebrate Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) have low and declining populations and need conservation action. In general, species that are state Threatened or Endangered, experiencing life history or habitat threats, are few or in low abundance/distribution, or showing declines in abundance or habitat become SGCN. They have been defined based in part on state and global ranks developed through Wisconsin's Natural Heritage Inventory (NHI) program and other criteria that address species or taxonomic characteristics not captured by the ranking method alone. As part of the 2015 Wildlife Action Plan, a formal, reproducible process to identify SGCN was established and again used in 2025.]
[TABLE:  A comprehensive table with SGCN/SGIN results for all animal and plant groups, including summarized notes, can be found in the 2025 WWAP collection for Element 1. 39 37 https://dnr.wi.gov/, search "NHI working list".]

[TABLE: 10 Results -Plant Species of Greatest Conserva�on Need Plant Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN)43  have low and declining populations and need conservation action. In general, species that are state Threatened or Endangered, experiencing life history or habitat threats, are few or in low abundance/distribution, or showing declines in abundance or habitat become SGCN. They have been defined based in part on state and global ranks developed through Wisconsin's Natural Heritage Inventory (NHI) program and other criteria that address species or taxonomic characteristics not captured by the ranking method alone. As part of the 2015 Wildlife Action Plan (WAP), a formal, reproducible process to identify SGCN was established and again used in 2025.]
