===== DOCUMENT PART: WWAP2025_E2-00_Habitat_Approach_Results.pdf =====

A natural community is an interactive assemblage of plants, animals and other organisms, their physical environment, and the natural processes that affect them. Environmental factors such as soil type, bedrock type, moisture level, slope, slope aspect, climate, and the natural disturbance regime play a key role in determining a species' ability to survive there. Natural communities occur in patterns on the landscape, range in size and change over time. Natural community classifications help us process and interpret these factors and relationships at larger landscape and smaller species population levels. Similar collections of species occur under similar environmental conditions.
Habitat is the combination of environmental factors that provide food, water, cover and space that a living thing needs to survive and reproduce. A habitat is an ecological or environmental area that plant and animal species inhabit and interact with. Like natural communities, habitat is also made up of physical factors such as soil, moisture, range of temperature, and availability of light as well as biotic factors such as the availability of food or nutrients and the presence of predators.
Natural communities and habitat consider similar factors, albeit from different perspectives. Still, natural community characteristics and the corresponding Natural Heritage Inventory community types are the most comprehensive way available to represent habitat for SGCN.

## Natural Communities are treated as equivalent to habitat in the Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan (WWAP).

Conservation planning for vertebrates can be done at the habitat, landscape, and ecoregional scales. Planning at these scales, however, can overlook the needs of many invertebrates and plants, which often have specific microhabitat requirements that cannot be addressed adequately at these scales. Nevertheless, to include all our state's biodiversity in conservation planning in the WWAP, habitat for invertebrates and plants has also been associated with natural community types.
Association scores are used to identify habitats and community types essential to the conservation of SGCN. An SGCN's habitat may intersect multiple natural community types, which are distributed across the landscape. The degree to which an SGCN or group of SGCN depends on natural community types for all or part of their life history needs (habitat) helps to identify and describe threats and conservation actions. The distribution of SGCN across ecological landscapes distinguishes where opportunities for conservation exist. Some of the best places on the landscape to carry out conservation action are within relatively diverse and intact or restorable natural community assemblages that support or can support multiple SGCN. The relationship between habitat and community types across the landscape that are essential to the conservation of SGCN and Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN) are assessed through SGCN-Natural Community, SGCN-Ecological Landscape and Natural Community-Ecological Landscape association scores.

## E2.2 Methods, Criteria and Process to Identify SGCN Habitats and Community Types E2.2 Habitat and Natural Communities Used in the 2025 WWAP

With some exceptions, natural community types follow the Wisconsin Natural Heritage Inventory (NHI)
foot_0
 types, which are defined by the assemblage of different plant and animal species that live together in a particular area, at a particular time. The NHI list of communities was used for this analysis because it can be cross walked to a standardized national system
foot_1
 for describing vegetative communities and has been used to inventory natural communities in Wisconsin since the mid-1980s. Community types are organized into groups in the WWAP that are broader than the NHI communities on the biodiversity webpages.
The list of habitat or natural 3 community types used in the 2025 WWAP has stayed largely the same since 2015, except that talus forest, American lotus lily marsh and rich northern mesic Element 2 Habitats and Community Types 9/29/2025 
[Draft]
 Wisconsin Department of Natural Resources Page 3 of 22 forest were added for a total of 111 communities. Rich northern mesic forest was split from the more common northern mesic forest due to the physical ecological and biological diversity that establishes its conservation significance. Association scores for this type will be like northern mesic forest but some SGCNs will score higher in this natural community.
Ephemeral ponds were identified as a "natural community type" in the 2015 WWAP. It has been moved to "other features" in the NHI database. Although still treated as a natural community type for updating association scores, the move recognizes that they support plant and animal assemblages (e.g., plants often associated with wetlands, amphibians and invertebrates), and this, together with their physical characteristics, result in a small, but unique ecosystem.

## E2.2.2 Condition and Relative Location of Habitat and Natural Community Types

Descriptions of all natural community types essential to SGCN are provided in in E2.3.1.
Most natural communities in the 2025 WWAP are tracked in the NHI database, but a few are not (referred to as WWAP natural communities). Each description of NHI natural communities contains a map locating counties where one or more NHI element occurrences of that community have been recorded. WWAP community occurrences are not entered into the NHI database and are not mapped.
The following are community types recognized in the 2025 WWAP that are not tracked in the NHI system:
• except for surrogate grasslands that are tracked, the remaining anthropogenic communities (aspen birch, conifer plantation, riverine impoundment or reservoir and transportationutility corridors); and
• northern forest seral stages, which are all defined and tracked under the main forest type (i.e., northern dry forest, northern mesic forest and northern dry mesic forest); and
• some aquatic communities
foot_3
 .
Anthropogenic communities present mixed opportunities for SGCN and their habitat. They occupy significant portions of the landscape in close association with natural communities and it is prudent to consider conservation in these places for some animals and plants.
Descriptions of WWAP natural communities are also provided in E2.3.1.

## E2.2.3 SGCN, Natural Community and Ecological Landscape Association Scores

In the WWAP, natural communities are treated as equivalent to habitat and comprise one of the three basic elements (SGCN, Natural Communities and Ecological Landscapes) used to help identify priority conservation actions and locations in Wisconsin. The WWAP scores these relationships in three ways:

## Species-Ecological Landscape Associations (SGCN x EL score)

Species' geographic distributions are compared across Wisconsin's sixteen ecological landscapes. Ecological Landscapes are delineated by associated landform groups that share common physical, biological, vegetation, geological, soil, water and climatic conditions. They are used to identify the best areas of the state to manage for different natural communities, key habitats, aquatic features, and native plants and animals using an ecosystem management perspective.

## Species-Natural Community Associations (SGCN x NC score)

SGCN are also assigned scores for their level of association with Wisconsin natural community types. Natural community types are considered representative of habitat for SGCN. SGCN-NC scores consider habitat requirements that would not be adequately captured using Ecological Landscapes alone and are essential to identifying and describing threats and conservation actions.

## Natural Community-Ecological Landscape Opportunities (NC x EL score)

Different natural communities occur in different parts of the state and as a result there are different opportunities to sustain these communities in different Ecological Landscapes. "Sustain" means ensuring that a given natural community type will be present and has high potential to maintain its natural composition, structure, and ecological function over a long period of time (e.g., 100 years).

## Definition of Score Values

High/Major (Score=3): For species, the natural community or ecological landscape contains essential biological, physical and ecological habitat elements or features for the species, which must be present in quality and quantity to sustain the species in Wisconsin. The majority of known or estimated occurrences of the species are found in the community or landscape. For natural communities, a major opportunity for sustaining the natural community in the Ecological Landscape exists, either because many significant occurrences of the natural community have been recorded in the landscape or restoration activities in areas of historical occurrence are likely to be successful in maintaining the community's composition, structure, and ecological function over a long period of time. Conservation actions implemented in this natural community or ecological landscape may result in significant improvement in the factors used to identify SGCN (e.g., rarity, trend and threat factors used in S/G Ranks).
Moderate/Important (Score=2): For species, the natural community or ecological landscape contains some, but not all biological, physical and ecological habitat elements that support or help to support this species. For communities, one to several significant occurrences have been recorded in the ecological landscape and are important in sustaining the community in the state. Conservation actions implemented in this natural community may result in moderate improvement in the factors used to identify SGCN (e.g., rarity, trend and threat factors used in S/G Ranks).
Low/Present (Score=1): Species or community is (and/or historically was) minimally associated with the biological, physical and ecological characteristics of this natural community or landscape. Include opportunities that may be temporary or that provide connectivity between major/important natural communities. The community or landscape provides one or a few important habitat elements (usually related to food), particularly when they are adjacent to communities or landscapes assigned a value of 2 or 3. Examples: Lakes Michigan and Superior will be assigned a 1 for shorebirds that feed over the lake; many communities will be assigned a 1 for bats, which have a broad feeding range although bats will not roost in these areas.
None (Score=0): Species or community does not (and did not historically) occur or is highly unlikely to use this natural community or ecological landscape.
Scores are presented for each combination of SGCN and 111 natural communities (SGCN x NC scores) and each combination of SGCN and 16 ecological landscapes (SGCN x EL scores) in E2.3.4.
The four scores (0 to 3) can be used individually or together (referred to as "Sum of Scores" from 0 to 9) to help identify the most effective conservation actions and the environmental settings in the state where those conservation actions are likely to be the most successful. If a conservation action is targeted at SGCN and natural communities with moderate (2) or high (3) association scores within ecological landscapes where those SGCN and natural communities are also rated moderate or high, the conservation action is more likely to be effective in helping SGCN and their habitat.
A Sum of Scores (Ecological Priorities) tool was created to select and filter for any combination of SGCN, natural community and ecological landscape where the sum is >3 (Appendix E2, Item 1, Example Output Sum of Scores). Locations where the sum of scores is > 6 indicates an ecological priority. Applying the sum of scores to a site to locate ecological priorities in this manner is predicated on some knowledge of the species and natural communities that are present or likely to be present. Conversely one can filter for a particular natural community x ecological landscape and determine which species may potentially be present as suitable targets for surveys or conservation actions.
For example, a site assessment that identifies open grasslands, prairies or sparsely wooded barrens on a property may be a suitable conservation site for prairie vole. A barrens community with more open understory and less dense grass may be more suitable for prairie deer mouse, but primarily in the central sands and southwest grasslands ecological landscapes. If the barrens communities are found as patches within primarily forested landscapes in the north, then they are less likely to work as restoration targets for the prairie deer mouse. The scores help make decisions about matching conservation actions linked to SGCN mammals to the most appropriate species and natural community targets in an area.

## E2.2.4 Updating Association Scores for the 2025 WWAP

Taxon Team Review: The three types of scores assigned in the 2015 WWAP were carried over to the 2025 WWAP for review by the Taxon Teams. Scores for "new" SGCN, including plants which are new to the 2025 WWAP, or new Natural Communities were also estimated by the Taxon Teams. The NC x EL scores were reviewed by the Plants and Natural Communities Team.
For some species groups there are hundreds of Species x Natural Community combinations to score. Association scores were not assigned to SGIN.
Additional context for the three association scores as well as instructions for updating the scores were provided in a very detailed Staff Guide to Association Scores. This Guide provided far more detail than in the 2015 WWAP to help reduce inconsistencies in how the Teams scored associations. It also provided direct access to species and natural community occurrences and locations to help with scoring. The following concepts form the basis for the Guide:
1. Consider all life cycle phases. Species may use different natural communities (habitats) within different ecological landscapes during their life cycle and depend on them to different degrees. Species that undertake seasonal or diurnal migrations may have more "1's" than species that do not. On the other hand, ecological landscapes that support important stopover sites and the natural communities that dominate these sites may be scored as highly as breeding habitat for a bird SGCN.
2. Consider current conditions or historical trends. Do not score based on estimates of future trends.
3. For some species, we may be confident in baseline inventory and monitoring data and can rely more heavily on documented occurrences. For other species, particularly invertebrates, scoring may only be feasible based on estimated habitat preferences or what is known about associated species in the taxon.
4. Keep the evaluation simple to reflect the nature of the scores and to make it easier to update them in the future as the environment changes.
5. Spatially calculated scores, other georeferenced data, WWAP2 scores and knowledge will be used to update existing associations scores or assign new scores to SGCNs and natural communities.
6. For species that are widespread and use different habitats, Teams were asked to identify only the natural community types essential to the species life history, rather than anywhere they could be. This corrected a tendency in the 2015 WWAP to assign numerous high SGCN x NC scores for some species (e.g., bats that forage widely across the landscape), making it difficult to distinguish key natural community types.
External Review: To share natural community/habitat and ecological landscape association scores for proposed 2025 SGCNs the following items were created:
• instructions (Appendix E2, Item 2, Input Draft Association Score Instructions)
• formatted PDFs of the draft scores (Item 3, Example SGCN x Natural Community Scores) and Item 4, Example External SGCN x Ecological Landscape Scores)
These were posted on the WWAP web page (Appendix E2, Item 5, Web Posting for Association Scores). An email was sent to the same list of external experts invited to review SGCN/SGIN results. Input could be provided through any means (i.e., in the spreadsheet, email or through communication directly with the Taxon Team).

## Finalization of Scores:

Teams met again to finalize the scores, using comments from external reviewers. Teams also rechecked some scores that were flagged for the potential inconsistencies described below.
• Wetland SGCNs that had association scores with terrestrial or aquatic communities >2. Terrestrial species that had association scores with aquatic communities >2. This is to confirm that the other community groups are important for the respective wetland or terrestrial species.
• For a given SGCN, NC and EL: a species was highly associated with a natural community and ecological landscape, but the natural community was not highly associated with that landscape. No community opportunities in the same ecological landscapes (EL) as the SGCN (per the EL scores). Some community x EL scores, especially for scattered/unmapped communities, may not be reflective of all the opportunities (e.g., riverine impoundments, inland beaches), but some are very rare, range restricted communities that are not found in the same part of the state as the SGCN and are worthy of a double-check (e.g., wet prairie).
• Confirm SGCN x anthropogenic communities scores >2 are important for the SGCN's viability in the state. This was indeed the case with some SGCN reptiles (e.g., nesting sites in road rights-of-way) where truly native habitat had been degraded or reduced throughout large portions of their range.

## E2.2.5 Summary of Significant Changes to Element 2 from the 2015 WWAP

There were no significant changes in the basic approach to identifying habitat for SGCN or the methodology that was used. Notable but less significant changes to the approach taken for Element 2 in the 2025 WWAP versus the 2015 WWAP are summarized below.
• Three natural communities were added and there was some reorganization within community groups.
• There is more occurrence information from NHI and other spatial datasets to assist in identifying natural communities and ecological landscapes most important to SGCN.
• A detailed guidance was developed to improve consistency among Taxon Teams.
• Taxon Teams were provided a tool that facilitated updates to association scores by comparing scores among species with similar habitat needs and using NHI element occurrences to understand distribution within the state.
• After the first round of updates, cross-checks were completed to correct inconsistencies among the three scores. Teams believe that 2025 WWAP association scores are more accurate and consistent.
• We relied on email, online fillable forms and personal follow-up with experts as needed. Inperson team meetings with experts did not take place.
• A "Sum of (Association) Scores" tool was developed to help WWAP users identify ecological priorities most suitable for conservation. With some information about the characteristics of the site (i.e., which landscapes, communities and some of the species present), one can filter the appropriate factors and get an indication of conservation opportunity or conservation targets).

## E2.3 Results -Essential SGCN Habitats, their Condition and Location

Natural communities/habitats used in the 2025 WWAP are provided in a searchable table according to the community groups summarized and described below. 
5
 In the Natural Community list, NHI tracked communities link to a page that provides a description of the community, key environmental factors that influence them and Wisconsin (state) and global ranks, which indicate the status of the community. On each community page there is also a map showing counties where the natural community occurs. Aquatic and anthropogenic communities (except for surrogate grasslands) that are not tracked in NHI are directed to different web pages that provide a description but no mapping. State and global ranks are developed similarly to SGCN ranks.

## Aquatic

Wisconsin has diverse aquatic resources that support numerous species, communities, ecological processes, and human uses. In addition, many terrestrial species and processes are dependent on neighboring aquatic systems. The aquatic communities of Wisconsin include two Great Lakes, 14,000 inland lakes, and 33,000 miles of perennial streams and rivers. On a landscape scale, aquatic systems are an integral piece of an ecological continuum that includes upland terrestrial systems and transitional wetland areas. Aquatic communities also often serve as important recharge or discharge areas for groundwater.
Wisconsin's Great Lakes shoreline on Lakes Superior and Michigan is approximately 1000 miles long. The Lake Michigan shoreline is also the site of Wisconsin's highest population density and most of its industrial base. State waters include 1.7 million acres of Lake Superior and 4.7 million acres of Lake Michigan including Green Bay. About a third of Wisconsin's 11 million land acres and a third of its river miles drain to these two lakes. With Euro-American settlement in the early 1800s and continuing up to present times, aquatic resources have incurred numerous alterations and stressors. The physical attributes of our waterbodies have been altered through damming and channelization, dredging, replacement of shoreline vegetation with substrates or vegetation that suit human needs, and filling/draining of associated wetlands. Water quality has been diminished with inputs of sediment, nutrients, wastewater, and industrial pollutants. Aquatic biota has also been negatively impacted through overfishing, introduction of invasive species, and damage from recreational watercraft.
The Aquatic Group distinguishes natural community types to reflect their hydrology, depth, alkalinity, and landscape position.

## Bare Soil and Bedrock

This group captures those natural communities that have few common themes that would allow them to fall into one of the other existing broad natural community groupings. Many of the communities within this group contain highly specialized flora and fauna reflective of the unique environment that they are dependent upon. Seeps, ice, wave action, water level fluctuation and bedrock-shaped, these communities support many rare, sometimes regionally endemic species. Included are the more common dry cliff and Great Lakes beach, the ecologically diverse forested ridge and swale, and the extremely rare, highly specialized algific talus slope. Several other, often obscure or lesser-known natural communities, are also included within this group.

## Barrens and Savannas

Barrens are plant communities that occur on sandy soils and are dominated by grasses, low shrubs, small trees, and scattered large trees. Curtis (1959) described these communities as pine barrens in northern and central Wisconsin and as oak barrens in southern and west-central Wisconsin. Because of their dynamic nature and the variability in structural types and species composition, they are difficult to describe and classify. Prior to Euro-American settlement, the vegetative structure of large barrens landscapes was quite variable and dynamic. Inclusions of variously sized and aged forest stands such as mature red pine, mature oak (bur, red, Hill's, or black), aspen groves, and numerous wetlands were typical of most pine and oak barrens.
Historically, Wisconsin's most extensive barrens were in large areas of sandy glacial deposits, including outwash plains, lakebeds, and outwash terraces along rivers. Geographically, areas of extensive barrens were concentrated in the Northeast Sands, Northern Highlands, Northwest Sands, and Central Sands Ecological Landscapes. They were also found on outwash terraces along the Lower Wisconsin, Lower Chippewa and Mississippi Rivers.
In the Midwest, savanna is generally used to describe an ecosystem that was historically part of a larger complex bordered by the prairies of the west and the deciduous forests of the east. This complex was a mosaic of plant community types that represented a continuum from prairie to forest. Savannas were the communities in the middle of this continuum. dominant canopy trees of savanna, except for Cedar Glade, where eastern red cedar is dominant).
Because savannas grade into both prairie and forest, there are no clear dividing lines between savanna and these two communities. In classifying the plant communities of Wisconsin, Curtis (1959) was forced to set limits for what he called savanna. He ultimately defined it as having no less than one tree per acre and no more than a 50% tree canopy. The more wooded part of the historical prairie-forest complex (with 50%-100% tree canopy) is known to us only through the early accounts of explorers and settlers. This community was already so distorted by lack of fire and other disturbances by the mid-1900s that it was not even classified and studied as a separate community by Curtis and his students. Recent research is now starting to shed some light on this plant community. This more heavily wooded portion of the prairie-forest complex is described here as oak woodland. Oak Barrens is another type of savanna that occurs on sandy soils.
Oak savanna now shares equal billing with tallgrass prairie as the most threatened plant community in the Midwest and among the most threatened in the world. Intact examples of oak savanna vegetation are now so rare that less than 500 acres are listed in the Natural Heritage Inventory as having a plant assemblage similar to the original oak savanna. This is less than 0.01% of the original 5.5 million acres.

## Northern Forests

Wisconsin's northern forest communities are found north of the vegetative Tension Zone, an area of climatic transition where the prairies and oak savannas that historically dominated southern Wisconsin changed to mixed deciduous-coniferous forests. Today, vegetation still changes along the Zone, but the transition is largely from agricultural uses to a more continuous forest cover. The shorter growing season and other environmental differences in northern Wisconsin makes this area less suitable for agriculture and allows forest to predominate.
Data from the Forest Inventory and Analysis Program indicate that in 2018 there were approximately 9.8 million acres of forest in the northern region of the state. Northern forests make up 60% of the total forested area statewide. Maple-beech-birch is the most common cover type group among northern forests, followed by aspen-birch. Lesser components included the oak-hickory, spruce-fir, pines, and lowland hardwood groups.
Forest ecosystems were drastically altered between the 1850s and early 1930s when nearly all of the primary forest was harvested or burned during the Cutover. Pine logging began near large rivers as early as the 1830s. Starting around 1870 and continuing into the 1920s, fires had a major effect on the northern forest, occurring with greater frequency and intensity due to slash left from logging and abundant new sources of fire that came with growing human populations. By the turn of the century, pulp mills were constructed to utilize the less-desirable wood, beginning the gradual switch to a pulp-dominated industry. Public reaction to the abuses of the Cutover resulted in legislation and government programs designed to rehabilitate the impacted forests.

## Prairies and Grasslands

Prairies and grasslands are characterized by a relative lack of trees and tall shrubs and are dominated by grasses, sedges, and forbs. They occur on a wide variety of topographies, soil types, and moisture regimes -from water-covered peat to the driest sandy soils. For this report, the term grassland refers collectively to several native vegetation community types including remnant prairie and bracken grassland.
Prairies are located mostly in the southern and western parts of the state and are divided into six different types that reflect the varying substrates on which they occur, from dry (sandy or otherwise) to "mesic" (intermediate soil moisture) to wet. Over 400 species of native vascular plants are characteristic of Wisconsin prairies, and most are restricted to prairie and savanna community types.
Bracken grasslands occur as openings on nutrient poor, sandy uplands in the otherwise forested regions of northern Wisconsin. Although like prairie in structure, bracken grassland is floristically very different (Curtis 1959), with bracken fern being a dominant species.
Tallgrass prairies are among the most decimated and threatened natural communities in the Midwest and the world. Of the 2.1 million acres (6% of state land area) that were native prairie when Europeans arrived in Wisconsin 150 years ago, less than 10,000 acres of varying quality (<1 % of state land area) native prairie remains today.

## Southern Forests

Wisconsin's southern forest communities occur south and west of the climatic Tension Zonethe approximate area where vegetative communities change from the prairie, savanna, oak, and mixed hardwood forests of the south to the mixed deciduous-coniferous forests of the north. The landscape in northern Wisconsin was largely forested prior to Euro-American settlement, but the southern forests were interspersed with extensive prairie and savanna communities.
Although several species range across both the northern and southern forests, there are floristic elements specific to each region (e.g., boreal elements in the north and prairie elements in the south). Also, species abundance may differ, and they may occur in different assemblages.
Southern Wisconsin's landscapes have changed greatly during the past 150 years. The loss of forest has been widespread in areas suitable for agriculture, industrial and residential development. Another major change occurred as the open landscapes of prairie and savanna succeeded to closed canopy forest following the exclusion of grazing and periodic fires. In many areas, canopy composition is now shifting from oak dominance to shade-tolerant dry-mesic/ mesic hardwoods, primarily due to the absence of disturbance. Land use patterns have resulted in significant forest fragmentation throughout southern Wisconsin, concentrating area sensitive SGCN in the few remaining large, forested blocks.
Data from the Forest Inventory and Analysis Program indicate that in 2018 there were approximately 3.4 million acres of forest in the southern region of the state. Northern forests make up 21% of the total forested area statewide. Oak-hickory is the most common forest type group, followed by the maple-beech-birch group, lowland hardwoods, pines, aspen-birch, and lowland conifers.
foot_5

## Wetland

Wetland communities have a common characteristic -their soil is periodically saturated with or inundated by water. A wetland is defined in the Wisconsin Statutes as "an area where water is at, near, or above the land surface long enough to be capable of supporting aquatic or hydrophytic vegetation and which has soils indicative of wet conditions".
Wetlands form where the shape of the land is conducive to retaining water, including flat areas or depressions with limited outflow, where groundwater is present at the land surface, and in floodplains with water flow-through. Wetlands can sometimes form in unlikely places, such as on slopes, when the local climate produces continually wet conditions (Verry 1988). Landscape features and other variables that vary from site-to-site will influence both ecological function and plant and animal diversity.
Wetlands are part of the water cycle of all ecosystems, and their location in the landscape allows them to function as a buffer between upland areas and surface waters 
(Weller 1981)
.
Wetlands perform several natural functions that benefit natural ecosystems and society. Water quality is often dependent upon wetlands because they serve to trap sediment, remove nutrients, protect shorelines, and slow the effects of flood water. They also serve as both discharge and recharge areas for groundwater and provide habitat for many species of plants and animals 
(Stearns 1978)
. In part due to these functions, wetlands exhibit higher biological productivity than most other community types and support rare biota. Forty-three percent of all federally-listed threatened and endangered species use wetlands at some point in their life cycles 
(Feierabend 1992)
. In Wisconsin, 32% of the state's listed species are wetland dependent. Further loss or degradation of wetlands would affect a disproportionate share of Wisconsin's rare species.
Disturbance and other factors have opened many wetlands to invasion by non-native invasive species that can reduce the ecological value of wetlands. 

## E2.4.2 SGCN x EL Scores

The SGCN x Ecological Landscape Scores are provided for all species in one table 8 . All sixteen ecological landscapes 9 are arranged horizontally across the top and all animal and plant SGCNs arranged vertically by group.
In this table all SGCNs are present since all SGCNs are present or associated with at least one ecological landscape.
Mammal Scores: For mammals, the SGCN x NC scores tend to illustrate that some species are very mobile and use many different natural communities (e.g., bats), while others use fewer community types (e.g., woodland vole or American marten). All mammal SGCNs are highly associated with at least two natural communities in our state. Many mammal SGCN are distributed in many of the ecological landscapes, while the American marten, Eastern pipistrelle and prairie vole are associated with fewer than half of the ecological landscapes in our state.
Bird Scores: For birds in particular, the SGCN-NC scores illustrate that birds utilize every natural community in the state, making them a key indicator of community health and function.
For example, while piping plover is highly associated with dunes and beaches, those along the Superior Coastal Plain Ecological Landscape consistently have the width preferred by plovers when compared to those present along the three Lake Michigan Coastal Ecological Landscapes. These scores help make decisions about matching conservation actions that are linked to SGCN birds to the most appropriate species and natural community targets in each area.
Fish Scores: Fish SGCN are predominantly associated with warmwater river and stream habitat.
A few species are associated with Great Lakes habitat and riverine lakes and ponds. Only one species, redfin shiner, is associated with multiple aquatic communities, albeit at low levels and in many cases, based on historically recorded occurrences.
One can see a marked contrast with the SGCN-EL scores versus the SGCN-NC scores in that many fish SGCN demonstrate relatively wide association across landscapes. This is in part because the boundaries of the ecological landscapes tend more toward "terrestrial" characteristics and simply because warm and cold-water habitats are relatively well distributed throughout the state. Four species have some association with ten or more of the sixteen landscapes. The most species are associated with the western coulee and ridges and southeast glacial plains landscapes because they are associated with the Mississippi River and Lake Michigan, respectively, which are part of those landscapes. Species are also associated with the ecological landscapes adjacent to the Wisconsin River.
Reptile and Amphibian Scores: The northern forest natural community group has the fewest number of associated herptile SGCNs (i.e., wood turtle, four-toed salamander and mink frog).
Few species are also associated with the "miscellaneous" natural community group, which is dominated by communities specific to the Great Lakes area and influenced by geologic or bedrock characteristics. Many herptile SGCN are present in dry open communities. In fact, similar reptile assemblages are associated with savanna, barrens and grassland community groups. Several herptile SGCNs are associated with wetland communities that have low or no association with the drier, open communities (e.g., mink frog) and some are not associated with wetlands (e.g., timber rattlesnake). But many reptiles and amphibians are present in multiple aquatic, wetland and terrestrial habitats because of their complex life history needs. It is also important to note that some SGCN have a relatively higher association with transportation and utility corridors because they have a wider range or diurnal or seasonal migrations through multiple habitats, which increases their interaction with corridors.
Four species (i.e., wood turtle, Blanding's turtle and to a lesser extent, four-toed salamander and pickerel frog) have associations with most of the ecological landscapes in our state. Six species have associations only with one or two ecological landscapes (i.e., prairie ring-necked snake, lined snake, gray ratsnake, queen snake, six-lined racerunner and Western worm snake).
All those are associated with the western coulee and ridges and southwest savanna landscapes, except for the queen snake, which is only associated with the southeast glacial plains and southern Lake Michigan coastal landscapes.
Invertebrate Scores: One can see a marked contrast with the SGCN-EL scores versus the SGCN-NC scores in that many aquatic invertebrate SGCN demonstrate relatively wide association across landscapes. This is in part because the boundaries of the ecological landscapes tend more toward "terrestrial" characteristics and simply because warm and cold-water habitats are well distributed throughout the state. While freshwater mussels are found in lake systems, all the mussel SGCNs are associated strongest with warmwater rivers and streams. Many terrestrial snail SGCNs are highly associated with moist-wooded cliffs, which are more numerous in the Niagara Escarpment and the Western Coulee and Ridges Ecological Landscape. Many of the terrestrial insect SGCNs are highly associated with barrens and grasslands systems.
Warm water habitats, and particularly those associated with major river systems are present throughout the state. However, they demonstrate considerable variation in habitat characteristics and therefore, conservation opportunity. The habitat needs of aquatic species in terms of vegetation, water flow, depth and quality vary on a scale that is not well-captured at the scale association scores are assigned. Nevertheless, they help us make decisions about matching conservation actions that are linked to invertebrate SGCN to the most appropriate species and natural community targets in an area.
Plant Scores: Dry prairie has the highest number of SGCNs with moderate or high degrees of association followed by southern mesic forest. Then there are a several mesic or wet natural 

## E2.4.3 NC x EL Scores

The NC x Ecological Landscape Scores are provided for all species in one table 
10
 . All sixteen ecological landscapes are arranged horizontally across the top. All natural community types are arranged vertically by group. In this table all Natural Community types are present since all are associated with and present conservation opportunities in at least one ecological landscape.

## E2.4.4 Using the Scores (Sum of Scores)

These associations are estimates based on expert and professional knowledge, and like the SGCN list itself, new information and changes in our environment are good reasons to reassess these scores periodically. Subtle shifts in natural community characteristics may render the natural community or habitat it represents less (or more) favorable for these species. While these scores can be used individually to some extent, they are best considered together with NC x EL association scores to make decisions about conservation actions at different scales.
The Sum of Scores
foot_8
 (Ecological Priorities) tool allows users to combine all three scores to help identify priority conservation targets, depending on the objectives of the user. The tool is described in 2.3.3 above and the linked spreadsheet contains thorough instructions for its use and interpretation.

By contrast, the Northwest Lowlands, Northwest Sands, Northeast Sands and Northern Highland landscapes have the fewest numbers of plant SGCNs associated with them. ===== DOCUMENT PART: WWAP2025_E3E4_Approach-Threats-Actions.pdf =====

Conservation actions and the threats they address are assigned to one or more threat categories and one or more conservation action categories based on a classification (or taxonomy) following national and international conventions recommended for SWAPs: Conservation Standards Threats Classification V.4 and Actions Classification V.2.
foot_1
 This is the same taxonomy used to evaluate the threats factor in NatureServe's rank calculator. The Conservation Measures Partnership classifications are typically applied to species, but they can be used for ecological systems as well. They have been adapted to natural communities/habitats in the WWAP. Establishing a common nomenclature and language for discussing conservation issues and actions makes it easier for people to plan efforts and compare outcomes. A Threats and Conservation Actions Report following these classifications has been developed for each of sixteen plant, animal and natural community groups.
Conservation Opportunity Areas (COAs) are also part of Element 4. They are the best places in Wisconsin to implement conservation actions in the Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan (WWAP) to
The reports were presented to Taxon Teams and external experts for review. The same experts contacted for SGCN/SGIN and association score reviews were, once again, invited to review the Threats and Actions reports during May and June 2025. Invitations were sent by email and instructions were provided online with the reports (Appendix E3-4, Item 1, Instructions for Threats and Actions Review).
Task 2. Compile Input and Update Threats and Actions Reports. Input was sorted by animal, plant or natural community group and integrated into the appropriate reports. Each report describes the major threats for that group. Each threat is followed by conservation actions appropriate to that threat. Threats and Actions Reports rely heavily on expert knowledge and other secondary sources and are not intended to be a review of the published literature for a particular threat or conservation action.
There are 16 Reports produced for the 2025 WWAP, reflecting the addition of plants, creation of a separate report for Anthropogenic Communities, and splitting the Miscellaneous Communities Report into Great Lakes and Bedrock Bare Soil Community Groups.
The nomenclature for the classification of threats
foot_3
 to SGCN and their habitats is based on the source of the impacts that a conservation target may be exposed to. They are the proximate activities or processes that impact SGCN or the habitat being assessed (e.g., unsustainable harvest practices). Threats lead to effects or stressors, which act upon the species, habitat, natural community or system (e.g., genetic erosion due to over harvesting). The source of the threat may be intimately mixed with and difficult to distinguish from its effects on the conservation target. Threats can be past (historical, unlikely to return or historical, likely to return), ongoing, and/or likely to occur in the future.
The nomenclature for the classification of actions includes direct and indirect responses to threats and their effects.
Because of the complexity inherent in describing each threat, one threat may be assigned to multiple corresponding CMP threat categories and multiple conservation action categories.

## E3-E4.2.2 Conservation Opportunity Areas

For the 2025 WWAP a team of NHC staff with diverse expertise in natural communities, invasive species and spatial databases was convened to discuss the scope of updates and improvements to COAs.
Initial discussions and interviews with other NHC staff yielded the following areas of improvement for COAs:
• The overall definition and characteristics of a COA can be made clearer and relevant to 2025. • It is important for users to readily access information that tells them why an area is a COA.
• It is important for users to receive some guidance or be aware of the most important actions to implement at a COA to benefit SGCN and their habitat. • There are different perspectives on the role of COAs, particularly boundaries and how rigorously COA boundaries should be defined. • Many more spatial datasets and software tools, including Midwest models, exist that can support local expertise and field verification to delimit biological and landscape characteristics that define COAs.
The scope for updating COAs for the 2025 WWAP included the following tasks.
Task 1. Update the definition and criteria for COAs. The team updated the general definition of a COA and the criteria originally described in the 2008 Implementation Plan that followed the 2005 WWAP
foot_4
 :
• presence of high priority Natural Community (uncommon, unique or declining) and/or multiple Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN); • representative and significant ecological features on a state, regional, continental or global scale; • identified as a priority conservation site in other initiatives or plans (e.g., Land Legacy Report); • establishes an interconnected network; and • large, minimally-fragmented, ecologically functioning systems.
In this task, the team did not attempt to redefine the criteria as quantitative standards by which an area could be mapped as a COA or not. This would be part of a larger effort to model COAs and their boundaries. Instead, they chose to add clarity to the ecological importance of each characteristic to aid in understanding why an area is a COA.
Task 2. Update Significant Ecological Features. Significant ecological features are driven by geodiversity (i.e., physical elements like landforms, bedrock, soil and topography) and further distinguished by the natural community types that establish because of those elements. Each COA is associated with one or more features. These were updated by NHC Ecologists for the 2025 WWAP to reflect current terminology and knowledge, make the descriptions more precise and provide place examples.
Task 3. Improve and update the COA database. The database contains attributes describing COAs (Appendix E3-4, Item 2, Example from COA Database. The information summarized below was updated or added to the database to highlight their overall ecological importance and their significance to SGCNs as opportuni�es for conserva�on.
Because many COAs overlap with properties managed by NHC Ecologists, they were asked to contribute their expertise to updating portions of the COA database. For this purpose, an Input Form was created for the Conservation Opportunity Area Database in Microsoft Access for DNR-NHC for staff to provide input to some fields of the database, particularly site descriptions and conservation actions. For example, the list of Natural Community EOs was reviewed by NHC staff to include WWAP habitats or communities with opportunities not reflected in the NHI database. Staff were provided with instructions for providing input (Appendix E3-4, Item 3 Instructions for NHC COA Review). There was no external expert input period for COAs like that for SGCN/SGIN, association scores, threats and actions. This is in part, because COAs are strongly associated with DNR managed properties (i.e., at least partially overlapping) and comment on new COAs, or boundary changes was not solicited for the 2025 WWAP. Improvements to COAs were included with the broad public review version of the 2025 WWAP.
Task 4. Update actions to implement at each COA. The COA Database Input Form provided an opportunity for staff to contribute examples of conservation actions that could be conducted in the COA. This list of actions is meant to be dynamic and informed by actions and input implemented by landowners and partners that benefit SGCNs and their habitats. Actions are verified against the Conservation Threats and Actions Reports (Elements 3 and 4) for each animal, plant and natural community group.
Task 5. Allow for obvious boundary changes. The COA Database Input Form allowed staff to recommend obvious boundary changes. Current COA boundaries were never intended to be precise; however, changes in land use over time and the methods that were used to draw them 6 Sources of information include: Task 6. Create COA reports for public use. Information in the database was exported to a formatted COA Report that will be available online as they are finalized. There are currently 193 COAs. Creating report content and presentation for each COA is time consuming. At the time of this 2025 WWAP submittal, three sample reports are provided in section E3-4.5. Ultimately all the COA reports will be posted to an online collection and the reports and COA attributes will be linked to the COA shapefiles for public use.

## E3-E4.3 Summary of Significant Changes to Elements 3 and 4 from the 2015 WWAP

There were no significant changes in the basic approach to identifying Threats and Actions and COAs or the methodology that was used.
Notable but less significant changes to the approach taken for Elements 3 and 4 in the 2025 WWAP versus the 2015 WWAP are summarized below.

## Threats and Actions

• Conservation action categories were updated to the current CMP V.2.
• Threats classifications in the reports are more explicit, following the current CMP V.4.
• Some Threats/Actions Reports were reorganized or added (e.g., Plants).

## Conservation Opportunity Areas

• COA shapefiles are now shared in the DNR Open Data Portal.
foot_6
• Overall definition and characteristics (criteria) for a COA were updated.
• Significant Ecological Features (SEF) were updated to our current knowledge and interpretation of Wisconsin's landscape and place examples were added to each feature. Rather than COAs matching to only one SEF, each COA can be associated with multiple SEF. • Attributes in the COA database was enhanced and updated (e.g., spatial intersects for SGCN and natural communities, tribal boundaries, and species with moderate or high SGCN x NC and SGCN x EL scores for locations where the overlapped NC and EL occurrences were added to the COA database and the COA Reports). An Access database form was created to provide input. • Individual COA Reports will be developed for each of the 193 COAs.

## E3-E4.4 Results -Threats and Actions

Sixteen Threats and Actions Reports organized by plants, animal and natural community group are provided in the 2025 WWAP, E3-4 Threats and Actions folder. Each Report describes the priority threats for that group followed by conservation actions. Each combination of threat(s) and action(s) is identified by the most current CMP Threats V.4 and Actions V.2 categories. Some threat categories are pervasive across many plant, animal and natural community groups: The following are sources the provide a broad and comprehensive assessment that supports the content of multiple Reports and are best accessed directly.
Effects of Climate Change on Midwest Ecosystems: Report series from the Midwest Climate Adaptation Science Center. Reports address the effects for 22 priority habitats found in the Midwest. Each report provides a process description explaining how communities and wildlife were selected to represent the Midwest followed by a careful compilation and analysis of current and projected climate stressors and habitat impacts. State Natural Areas Strategic Plan 14 : This report provides the framework for developing specific management recommendations for State Natural Areas. The threats and stressors described in the report parallel those described in the 2025 WWAP.

## E3-E4.5 Results -Conservation Opportunity Areas

## E3-E4.5.1 Updated Definition

Conservation Opportunity Areas (COAs) are the best places in Wisconsin to implement conservation actions in the Wisconsin Wildlife Action Plan (WWAP) to protect and conserve SGCNs and their habitat. They are places on the landscape with significant ecological features, natural communities or SGCN habitats for which Wisconsin is responsible for protecting and conserving. Focusing conservation actions in these prioritized areas can increase the likelihood of long-term success, maximize effectiveness over larger landscapes, improve funding efficiency, and promote voluntary cooperative efforts across ownership boundaries.

## E3-E4.5.2 Updated COA Criteria

These criteria are the same as those used to define COAs for the 2005 WWAP and Implementation Plan. They have been updated with more current and concise language. Each COA fulfills one or more of these criteria.

## Table E3-E4.1 Criteria for Conservation Opportunity Areas

Conservation Opportunity Area Criteria Significant ecological features present. Landscape level groupings or combinations of Natural Community and physical characteristics that collectively exhibit distinct attributes. Recognition of these features focuses conservation not only on biological targets (species and natural communities) that may rearrange in response to a changing climate, but also on the physical factors that create diversity in the first place. Combinations of bio-and geodiverse areas. A significant ecological feature is unique to Wisconsin, or a relatively significant portion of the feature exists within the state.

## Conservation Opportunity Area Criteria

Presence of large populations and/or concentrations of SGCN. Sites with large populations of one or more SGCN, multiple SGCN, or high concentration of SGCN offer relatively more opportunity to benefit SGCNs. Even if populations or species diversity are declining, this assumes at least moderate viability such that conservation actions within the COA can help to sustain the species over time. While this is intended to limit single-species conservation, sites that support the few remaining populations of species, regardless of their viability should be considered. These characteristics can help to define the boundary of the COA. Presence of natural communities or habitats that support SGCN. Natural communities within the site are associated with known or probable SGCNs (e.g., based on association scores). Site quality contributes to the ecological function of a site and includes vegetation structure, composition and, for natural communities, the degree of hydrologic or soil disturbance. For restoration and enhancement to be feasible and sustainable (i.e., successful), natural communities exhibit low to moderate degradation, low to moderate environmental stressors. Identified as a priority conservation site in other initiatives or plans. Recognizing other initiatives or plans in sync with the definition and purpose of COAs, including relevant threats and actions, adds weight to their relevance as a conservation target. Other plans, maps or initiatives may overlap other COA criteria (e.g., priority natural communities). Contributes to a network of climate resilient cores and corridors. This criterion emphasizes how resilient the site is to climate change and whether it has features of landscape connectivity important for sustaining a climate resilient ecosystem. Resilient sites are more likely to maintain biological diversity and ecological function as climate change occurs. This includes sites that have potential for greater resiliency through additional management. These "biodiversity strongholds" have more variable topography and are more locally connected by natural land cover than other sites with similar soils/underlying bedrock. Such geodiverse sites have been correlated with high rare species richness and are useful tools for conserving both rare and common species. Sustaining biological diversity and ecological processes across the region are more likely if the resilient sites are embedded in a larger network of connected lands that allow for dispersal and movement between sites. Conservation efforts may be more successful at sites that are already moderately or highly resistant or resilient against climate change. Large, minimally fragmented, ecologically functioning system. This criterion focuses on the ability of a site to readily support and maintain ecological processes and a diverse community of organisms in the face of inputs and disruptions in addition to climate change, based on size and lack of fragmentation. Terrestrial ecosystems are complex combinations of plants, animals, soils, and other abiotic factors that provide critical ecological benefits, such as water quality improvement, flood control, carbon storage, climate regulation, aesthetic enjoyment, and biodiversity. Sites with moderate to high ecological integrity provide valuable information for assessing condition and conservation opportunity and effectiveness. The structure, composition, function, and connectivity of the ecosystem continue to operate within the bounds of natural disturbance regimes.

## E3-E4.5.3 Significant Ecological Features

Landscape features that help to define Wisconsin's geological, ecological and biological diversity are described in the Significant Ecological Features (SEF) report in the 2025 WWAP Collection, E4. Opportunity Areas (COA). These features provide a cohesive basis for identifying Each SEF is assigned to a geographic scale of uniqueness based on common expert knowledge: global, continental, Midwest-regional or state. These categories can assist users to communicate priorities in different contexts. Place examples are provided for each SEF.

## E3-E4.5.4 COA Summary Spreadsheet

A downloadable spreadsheet with the following information for all 193 COAs is provided in the 2025 WWAP Collection, E4. Opportunity Areas (COA). Definitions and sources for each attribute and a link to each Ecological Landscape/COA map are provided in the first tab of the spreadsheet. An abbreviated PDF list of Conservation Opportunity Areas by Ecological Landscape is provided in Appendix E3-4, Item 4 for ease of reference.
• COA Name • COA # where first two digits are numbered 1 to 16 corresponding to each of the ecological landscapes, followed by the sequentially numbered COA in that ecological landscape. COA reports contain information compiled in the COA database that includes the attributes in the abovementioned COA spreadsheet, a map and confirmed and probable species and natural community lists. These reports satisfy the goal of allowing users to understand why a site is a COA and what conservation actions they may consider. It is our hope that conservation actions will be periodically updated from partners, landowners and land managers in the database and the reports. Compilation of these reports for all 193 COAs is a careful and complex task that is outside the scope of this revision. Reports will be completed and posted to the 2025 WWAP website as they are completed. Many states have identified the best places to implement conservation for SGCN and their habitats. While the reasons are straightforward, the process and the criteria used to identify priority sites for conservation are complex, varied and controversial. When viewing the reports, differences in how COAs are defined and identified for the 2025 WWAP and other states or conservation plans should be acknowledged.
Wisconsin COAs are weighted toward public state-owned properties or sites where the WDNR has a partnership role. Naturally, we have the most information about these areas with respect to the criteria described in E3-4.5.2. More importantly, these sites are protected in perpetuity and as such are permanent reservoirs of biodiversity.
COA boundaries are deliberately imprecise because the criteria used to identify them are general and largely come from large-scale data layers; the boundaries are not field verified. Not all the area within a COA presents the same level of opportunity for successful conservation; conversely, opportunities exist outside COA boundaries.
The species lists for individual COA reports (see below) are based on occurrences confirmed within a five-mile radius of the COA boundary and probable occurrences. The latter is based on the SGCN-Natural Community and SGCN-Ecological Landscape association scores and represent "potential" for the site. Species associated with the natural communities present in the COA or the landscape in which the COA may not be present, or they could be established by implementing conservation actions.
COAs are areas that we know have habitat value and are likely to have successful outcomes. It may result in underrepresenting sites that are important but need restoration or protection to realize their ecological value to SGCN and their habitat (e.g., restorable wetlands under the wetland mitigation program). Sites that meet the criteria of a COA certainly exist on private lands or other conservation properties that are not accessible. The SGCN-Natural Community, SGCN-Ecological Landscape and Natural Community-Ecological Landscape scores help individuals and organizations to recognize characteristics of potential COAs on their properties.

===== DOCUMENT PART: WWAP2025_E3E4_ThreatsActions01_MammalActions.pdf =====

Transportation and service corridors, and timber and wood harvesting were the most frequently cited threat during the mammals threat assessment. As with different types of development and agriculture, transportation and service corridors and timber or wood harvesting, can also result in loss of habitat, habitat fragmentation or reduced habitat quality.
The nature and extent of transportation and service corridors' effects on small mammals, not only depends on the footprint (i.e., its dimensions and location) as it often does with development, but how vegetation control, access and maintenance of the line or road are managed.
Timber harvest does not necessarily fragment forests on a landscape scale but can reduce habitat availability or quality for small mammals over multiple generations, depending on how timber harvests are planned and conducted.

## CMP Conservation Actions Classification M1

7.2 Policies & Guidelines 7.1 Laws, Regulations & Codes Among the conservation actions linked to these categories, industry sector management practices for the transportation, utility and forestry sectors are very important for sustaining ecological and habitat value for mammal SGCNs. The specificity of industry sector management practices and the degree to which they are optional, voluntary or required varies, depending on their policy or regulatory context. However, they generally include measures that give users the opportunity to integrate and maximize conservation benefits to SGCNs. Managing northern forests to benefit SGCN mammals would include maintaining a diverse tree composition, especially to favor conifer species, leaving downed woody debris to provide cover, allowing for smaller openings that fill in relatively quickly, and providing structurally diverse forests with well-developed ground layer, shrub and sub-canopy levels. Climate change and its effects were recorded as a major threat for 31 species during the mammal threat assessment. Many mammals are experiencing phenological mismatch (e.g., hares or weasels with a winter white coat during snow free, early spring conditions) due to climate change making them more vulnerable to predators. Some northern conifer forest community types are predicted to shift northward out of the state jeopardizing populations of species found in these forest types, at the southern edge of the range in Wisconsin. Additional impacts include warming of hibernation sites, extreme flood events, timing with insect hatches, and intolerance of high temperatures.

## CMP Conservation Actions Classification M2

1.1 Site/Area Stewardship Management actions should be undertaken to make northern coniferous plant communities more resilient and better able to adapt to changing climates. All northern plant communities are particularly susceptible and in need of management intervention. In addition to threats and actions discussed in this report, the 2020 Statewide Forest Action Plan
foot_2
 provides additional perspective for the forestry community and other conservation decisionmakers. Fire suppression, natural system modification and water management were cited as a major threat for 26 mammal species during the threat assessment. On many of our terrestrial landscapes, lack of fire has changed ecological processes in ways that affect most small mammal SGCN. Succession of grassland habitats to shrubland and woodland and changes in species composition due to lack of fire have altered habitat quality, including food sources, soil temperature and increase in predators, all of which adversely affect small mammal survival. Residential and commercial development and agriculture were cited as a major threat for 24 mammal species during the threat assessment. In the Open Standards for the Practice of Conservation classification, "development" includes the footprint of the activity on the landscape-all phases of construction and operation. Similarly, agricultural impacts consider the conversion of land for placement of agricultural activities and operation of the farm for animals, crops or grazing. All subcategories of residential, commercial and agricultural use and the transportation and utility corridors that link them can affect small mammal SGCN, but not always in negative ways. The footprint of transportation and utility corridors can expose mammals to predators and provide movement corridors. The relative significance of each of these sources of impact depends, in part, on where it occurs in the state and how closely the development is associated with natural community types that these species are found in (i.e., the siting and intensity of the development are important). For example, agriculture is a more important issue for small mammal SGCN in southwestern ecological landscapes, whereas commercial and residential development is more important in south central and south eastern ecological landscapes.

## CMP Conservation Actions

Many native mammals have declined both in range and abundance in the past 100 years due largely to converting native landscapes into other uses, which has the cumulative effect of habitat loss, fragmentation, and reduction in habitat quality. Habitat conversion and continued loss is a widespread threat facing mammal Species of Greatest Conservation Need in Wisconsin. Habitat alteration has three major components: loss of the original habitat, reduction in habitat patch size, and increasing isolation of habitat patches, all of which contribute to a decline in biological diversity within the original habitat 
(Wilcox 1980
, Wilcox & Murphy 1985)
. Specific examples of these issues affecting habitat vary widely, from conversion of native prairie habitat to row crops, roads, and housing 
(WDNR 2006)
 to changes in forest habitats through management activities that decrease the extent of older forest, coarse woody debris, or dramatically open the forest canopy (WDNR 2010a, WDNR 2010b). Given their relatively short dispersal capabilities small mammals are particularly susceptible to development that fragments habitat. Loss of these animals from an area makes it very difficult for them to recolonize unless a local source population exists nearby.

## CMP Conservation Actions Classification M4

6 mammals would include maintaining a diverse tree composition, especially to favor conifer species, allowing for smaller openings that fill in relatively quickly, leaving downed woody debris to provide cover, and providing structurally diverse forests with well-developed ground layer, shrub and sub-canopy levels. In southern Wisconsin, financial and nonfinancial incentives that protect or preserve land, regardless of ownership, in a natural state, as either native prairie, pasture, surrogate grassland, or hay and grains to maximize grassland acreage benefit our grassland small mammals. Many SGCN small mammals do not require remnant native prairie but do need larger tract of grass cover to avoid isolation since they can only move short distances. Actions to address this issue fall into a combination of awareness and education and developing natural methods of invasive and problematic species control and prevention. Avoid pesticide use that may impact SGCN mammal populations (diazinon). Limit use of chemicals and pesticides in grassland habitats because of known effects on reproduction and other aspects of mammal biology. Continuing to apply best practices for pesticide application ensures healthy habitats for mammal SGCN. Integrated pest management practices that consider natural biological processes and biopesticides, preventative cultural practices and emphasis on control are important components of these actions. Toxic effects of chemicals on bats species are well cited in the literature; studies on other small mammals like voles, mice and shrews are less common. A precautionary approach regarding application and use of chemicals in or near habitat occupied by mammal SGCN is prudent from a conservation perspective. Prevent and minimize the spread of invasive species by following best management practices
