## Elements 3 & 4. Threats/Issues and Conservation Actions Savanna Community Group

This Section describes issues and conservation actions that are common to all or most of the community types 1 in the savanna community group. As much as possible, the source of the threat is described as well as the stresses or effects that occur directly or indirectly as a result of the threat. Stresses are generally thought of as loss, conversion and/or degradation of the natural community. Distinguishing the source of the impact from the effects or the changes that occur to the community is important because the two typically need a different approach and set of conservation actions. Multiple sources of impact may have the same or similar effects on a community. Similar effects may be addressed collectively by a single action or suite of actions.
The savanna group includes the following four community types 2 :
• Oak Opening • Oak Woodland • Eastern Red-cedar Thicket (see also Southern Forest community group) • Bracken Grassland Hereafter, we will use "savanna" to refer to generally to the four types and "oak savanna" to refer to just oak opening and oak woodland.
Conservation actions for most or all savanna community types are organized according to categories in the Conservation Actions Classification based on the Open Standards threats and actions classification 3 . If the threat/issue and its associated conservation action(s) apply to one or a few species they are identified as such. Conservation actions overlapping in content or scope may be grouped under a single code.
More about how threats and issues or conservation actions were developed and how this information can be used to make conservation decisions can be found in E3-4 Threats & Actions of the 2025 WWAP collection.
1 Community or natural community is used in the WWAP as a proxy for habitat.
2 For descriptions of the community types, visit 
https://dnr.wi.gov
, search "biodiversity".
3 Threats and actions classifications developed by World Conservation Union (IUCN) and Conservation Measures Partnership. Threats classification 4.0 and Actions classification 2.0 can be download from: 
https://www.conservationstandards.org/library-item/threats-and-actions-taxonomies/
.

## Threat/Issue Sa1-Fire Suppression

Threats Classification Sa1
7.1 Natural System Management & Modifications -Fire & Fire Management
Historically, savannas were maintained primarily by frequent fires, either started by lightning strikes or by Native Americans who burned large areas to produce food for game or to aid in hunting and gathering activities. Fire is essential to Wisconsin's savanna communities for a variety of reasons: 1) It limits woody encroachment; 2) It stimulates early and robust growth of native plants; 3) It can deter growth of some non-native invasive and other problematic species; 4) It stimulates flowering and fruit production of native plants; and 5) It increases plant species diversity. Savannas in the absence of regular fire will succeed to woody species and will become less diverse over time. Climate change projections for Wisconsin suggest that the windows of opportunity for prescribed burning may be constrained due to changing conditions (e.g., extreme drought and heat, earlier spring green-up, frequent and intense storms).
Invasive and other problematic species can also limit the ability of managers to apply fire by reducing the amount of fuel available to carry fire and by creating a moister ground level microclimate. This is particularly true of brushy species.

## Conservation Actions Classification Sa1

1.2 Ecosystem & Natural Process (Re)Creation -Terrestrial 7.1 Laws, Regulations & Codes Maintain and create contiguous blocks of related fire-dependent communities that capture a complete gradient from grassland/open wetland to savanna to oak forest.

## Outreach & Communications -Negative perceptions

Work with Wisconsin's Prescribed Fire Council (prescribedfire.org) to make the use of prescribed fire safe, effective, and more broadly accepted as a management tool. Promote private lands prescribed fire application near existing public land fire dependent habitat blocks and seek private-public collaboration (such as fireline agreements) to create larger, more efficient burn units across property boundaries. 1.1 Site/Area Stewardship Take actions to facilitate rapid mobilization of prescribed burn crews (e.g., prepare units in advance of burn season).
Maximize impacts of limited burn seasons by burning larger units. Seek opportunities to reduce staff time and resource needs for monitoring large burn units for extended periods when perimeter is secure, but smoldering material exists interior of the unit. Especially in savanna and fire dependent forested habitats where heavy fuels are common.
At statewide level:
• Determine average annual Rx fire need to maintain priority fire dependent communities Evaluating the potential effects to invertebrates should be routinely considered in plans to use fire for restoration or management of this community type. The frequency, intensity and area burned should be planned considering the life history, habitat needs and distribution of firesensitive invertebrate species both on the subject property and adjacent habitat. In cases where burning is the preferred community management tool, but invertebrate species impacts are undetermined or potentially significant, the feasibility of creating refugia should be examined as should alternative methods for invasive, shrub and canopy management.
Quantify and monitor the positive and negative impacts that prescribed burning and other management activities undertaken in grassland, barrens and savanna communities have on SCGN invertebrates to improve management decisions and techniques and improve intended outcomes.

## Threat/Issue Sa2-Invasive Species

Threats Classification Sa2

## Invasive Non-Native Species

Non-native invasive plants are prolific reproducers in the absence of their homeland's natural checks and balances, and outcompete native plants by monopolizing light, water and nutrient resources. The most common non-native invasives of savannas include herbs such as garlic mustard (Alliaria petiolata) and shrubs such as common buckthorn (Rhamnus cathartica) and Eurasian bush honeysuckles (Lonicera spp.). Climate change scientists suggest that non-native invasive species may increase in productivity with increasing CO 2 , warmer temperatures, earlier springs, and reduced snowpack, and may invade new areas during extreme flood events. Some native woody species are also 'problematic' in savannas, especially in the absence of fire, and can outcompete native vegetation similarly to non-native invasives; climate change may exacerbate this threat as increased CO 2 and nitrogen deposition further stimulate growth of woody species.

## Conservation Actions Classification Sa2

Due to increased colonization and growth rates, multiple control actions used together should be utilized in invasive species control programs to create an Integrated Pest Management program (IPM).
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship 6.4 Conservation Planning At the site level, employ an eight-part approach to non-native invasive species: 1) careful planning; 2) prevention; 3) early detection and rapid response; 4) control; 5) slowing the spread; 6) reducing impacts; 7) monitoring; 8) restoration.
Restore sites to confer resistance to infestation by non-native invasives. This may involve restoring system functions (e.g., fire), restoring natural community structure (canopy, midstory, shrub layer), and ameliorating ground layer species.
Develop a plan by conducting surveys for prevention and rapid management of NR40 prohibited invasive species and management of low-density populations. Set reasonable management objectives based on this information. Consider designating management zones based on degree of infestation and available resources (zero tolerance, acceptable threshold, slow the spread).
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship-Prevention -Terrestrial (upland)
Prevent invasions of non-native plants by limiting human vectors (e.g., install boot brushes at trail heads, clean out seeds from maintenance equipment), minimizing soil disturbance (including during heavy equipment operations and using mowed and raked firebreaks or natural features instead of mechanically exposing mineral soil), maintaining healthy and diverse natural communities, conducting periodic inspections of high-risk areas (e.g., trails), and revegetating disturbed sites with native plants. Prevent and minimize the spread of invasive species by following best management practices
foot_0
 . Conduct regular monitoring of sites to detect new invasions and to evaluate the success of pest management plans and control measures.
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship-Invasive/problematic species control-Terrestrial (upland)
Control non-native invasive species and problematic woody species by manual, mechanical, and/or chemical means.
In areas where eradication and control are not feasible, slow the spread of non-native invasives into adjoining areas by restricting activities during certain seasons, minimizing travel through areas, and inspecting clothing and equipment.
If eradication, control, and containment methods fail to manage an infestation of non-native invasives, reduce their impact on elements of the natural community by focusing control efforts to allow for specific functions to occur (e.g., tree regeneration), translocating sensitive species, or accommodating unavoidable changes (e.g., if a common buckthorn infestation limits the ability to burn, use mowing and brushing as a surrogate for fire).
7 
(annuals, perennials, forbs, grasses, etc.)
. The vulnerability of savannas to climate change primarily stems from the exacerbation of threats that are already significant, especially non-native invasive species and woody invasion/mesophication. Lower winter snow depths may also render some savanna plants susceptible to frost and drought damage. The threat of tree pests and diseases may increase as trees become stressed with extreme heat, drought, and frost damage. Furthermore, prescribed burning opportunities may change due to extreme drought and heat, earlier spring green-up, and frequent and intense storms. The exact nature of these potential changes is currently unclear: windows of opportunity for burning may become narrower or they may shift to different seasons, conditions may result in cooler and less effective burns, opportunities may actually increase with warmer drier conditions, or conditions may become more volatile and trigger more regulatory constraints. These changes may vary depending on site conditions, landscape variables, and ecoregion. Managers may wish to adopt a proactive approach that offers them the most flexibility in the face of changing and unpredictable conditions.
Differences in projected vulnerabilities among the three savanna types stem in part from the relative fates of the dominant tree species. Suitable habitat for bur oak, black oak and shagbark hickory is projected to remain the same or increase with climate change; habitat for white oak may remain the same with "low change" but decrease with "high change." Overall, this bodes well for the dominant trees of Oak Opening. In contrast, under a "high change" scenario, two other species that are important to Oak Woodland, red oak and black cherry, may incur large decreases in suitable habitat. Suitable habitat for eastern red cedar is projected to see large increases with climate change, a favorable factor relating to Cedar Glade communities.
While Wisconsin's savannas have characteristics that may confer resistance to climate change, and while they may benefit from some changes such as increased temperatures and drought, potential negatives may outweigh these positives; the uncertainty relating to the future of prescribed fire may represent the fulcrum in this balancing act. An additional and significant consideration is the fact that Wisconsin's remnant savannas are already highly degraded and in need of consistent intensive management (especially prescribed fire). Since these communities are overall expected to be resilient to climate change, it is most critical for appropriate management now in order to ensure these communities are structurally and compositionally healthy to meet future climate conditions. Promote drought-and frost-tolerant species and plant morphologies through regular prescribed burning (mainly pertains to Oak Opening and Oak Woodland; the fire regime of Cedar Glade remains poorly understood).

## Conservation Actions

Impact of climate change on frost pockets, an important ecosystem driver for Bracken Grassland.
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship Take actions to facilitate rapid mobilization of prescribed burn crews (e.g., prepare units in advance of burn season).
Maximize impacts of limited burn seasons by burning larger units. Seek opportunities to reduce staff time and resource needs for monitoring large burn units for extended periods when perimeter is secure, but smoldering material exists interior of the unit. Especially in savanna and fire dependent forested habitats where heavy fuels are common.
At statewide level:
• Determine average annual Rx fire need to maintain priority fire dependent communities • Develop implementation plan to incrementally make progress towards meeting statewide Rx fire need, including new and existing partnerships, when resources become available or to seek outside resources such as grants.
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship 6.4 Conservation Planning At the site level, employ an eight-part approach to non-native invasive species: 1) careful planning; 2) prevention; 3) early detection and rapid response; 4) control; 5) slowing the spread; 6) reducing impacts; 7) monitoring; 8) restoration.
Note: Other conservation actions listed under the following threat/issues above and for other community groups can be adapted to consider the effects of changing temperature and precipitation: fire suppression; non-native and native invasive and problematic plants.

This is a summary of threats/issues and conservation actions that are common to all or most of the community types 1 in the southern forest community group. As much as possible, the source of the threat is described as well as the stresses or effects that occur directly or indirectly as a result of the threat. Stresses are generally thought of as loss, conversion and/or degradation of the natural community. Distinguishing the source of the impact from the effects that occur to or in the community is important because the two typically need a different approach and set of conservation actions. Multiple sources of impact may have the same or similar effects on a community. Similar effects may be addressed collectively by a single action or suite of actions.
The southern forest group includes the following 11 community types: Upland Southern Forests Descriptions for the southern forest community types can be found online. 
2
 Conservation threats and actions for most or all barrens community types are organized according to categories in the Conservation Measures Partnership
foot_1
 . If the threat/issue and its associated conservation action(s) apply to one or a few species they are identified as such. Conservation actions overlapping in content or scope may be grouped. In addition to threats 
1
 Community or natural community is used in the WWAP as a proxy for habitat. and actions discussed in this report, the 2020 Statewide Forest Action Plan
foot_3
 provides additional perspective for the forestry community and other conservation decisionmakers.
More about how threats and issues or conservation actions were developed and how this information can be used to make conservation decisions can be found in E3-4 Threats & Actions of the 2025 WWAP collection. Historically, the oak-and pine-dominated upland forests of southern Wisconsin were exposed to fire of variable frequency and intensity, either started by lightning strikes or by Native Americans. Fire is important to Wisconsin's southern upland forest communities for a variety of reasons: 1) it limits mesophication; 2) it can facilitate reproduction of key canopy species such as oak; 3) it can deter growth of some non-native invasive species; and 4) it increases plant species diversity. In the absence of regular fire, especially in areas showing impacts of deer herbivory, these forest types will succeed to more shade tolerant species and will become less diverse over time. Neither the extent or frequency of current prescribed fire implementation are sufficient to either meet treatment needs nor restore critical fire dependent ecosystem processes. Maximize impacts of limited burn seasons by burning larger units. Seek opportunities to reduce staff time and resource needs for monitoring large burn units for extended periods when perimeter is secure, but smoldering material exists interior of the unit. Especially in fire dependent forested habitats where heavy fuels are common.

## Most commonly cited threats included in this report:

## Conservation Actions Classification SF1

At statewide level:
• Determine average annual Rx fire need to maintain fire dependent communities • Determine gap between resources available and what is required to reach the average annual Rx fire need • Seek opportunities to utilize current resources more efficiently and effectively to improve achievements • Develop implementation plan to incrementally make progress towards meeting statewide Rx fire need, including new and existing partnerships, when resources become available or to seek outside resources such as grants.

## Basic Research & Status Monitoring-Research -Natural community threats and actions

Develop prescribed burn guidelines for pine relicts and oak regeneration, but with special consideration to current and future projected climatic conditions and past land use impacts.

## Threat/Issue SF2-Invasive and Problematic Species

Threats Classification SF2 

## Conservation Actions Classification SF2

1.1 Site/Area Stewardship 6.4 Conservation Planning At the site level, employ an eight-part approach to non-native invasive species: 1) careful planning; 2) prevention; 3) early detection and rapid response; 4) control; 5) slowing the spread; 6) reducing impacts; 7) monitoring; 8) restoration.
Develop a plan by conducting surveys for prevention and rapid management of NR40 prohibited invasive species and management of low-density populations. Set reasonable management objectives based on this information. Consider designating management zones based on degree of infestation and available resources (zero tolerance, acceptable threshold, slow the spread).

## Ecosystem & Natural Process (Re)Creation -Terrestrial

Restore sites to confer resistance to infestation by non-native invasives, pests and diseases. This may involve restoring system functions (e.g., fire), restoring natural community structure (canopy, mid-story, shrub layer), and ameliorating ground layer species.
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship Prevention -Terrestrial (upland)
Prevent invasions of non-native plants by limiting human vectors (e.g., install boot brushes at trail heads, clean out seeds from maintenance equipment), minimizing soil disturbance (including during heavy equipment operations and using mowed and raked firebreaks or natural features instead of mechanically exposing mineral soil), maintaining healthy and diverse natural communities, conducting periodic inspections of high-risk areas (e.g., trails), and revegetating disturbed sites with native plants. Prevent and minimize the spread of invasive species by following best management practices
foot_4
 .
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship -Invasive/problematic species control -Prevention -Terrestrial (upland)
Conduct outreach to prevent invasions of non-native earthworms via release of fishing baitworms or vermicomposting. 1.1 Site/Area Stewardship-Invasive/problematic species control -Control -Terrestrial (upland)
Control non-native invasive species and problematic woody species by manual, mechanical, and/or chemical means.
If eradication, control, and containment methods fail to manage an infestation of non-native invasives, reduce their impact on elements of the natural community by focusing control efforts to allow for specific functions to occur (e.g., tree regeneration), translocating sensitive species, or accommodating unavoidable changes. Explore options to mitigate earthworms and jumping worms impacts, such as fall prescribed fires shortly after leaf fall.
1 Blocks of habitat with understories dominated by dense invasive species separating areas of well-managed understories can functionally fragment habitat even if the overstory is similar. In addition, forested wetlands can be inadvertently converted to non-forested wetlands through forest management that causes takeover by reed canary grass or regeneration failure from deer browse. On the other side of the coin, some species require young forest, and a lack of management can be detrimental. A balanced approach that considers the need for large blocks of old forest as well as areas of mid-seral and young-seral forest is recommended.

## Conservation Actions Classification SF4

1 Extreme storms that cause wind damage, severe flooding, and excessive runoff are already on the rise and are projected to increase further 9 , particularly affecting lowland forests already under stress from emerald ash borer and reed canary grass. Climate change is also likely to increase the risk of invasive species, which are adapted to respond to disturbance and rapid environmental change. Finally, prescribed burning, sometimes used as a management tool in upland forests, may change due to periodic drought, earlier spring green-up, and frequent and intense storms. Managers may wish to adopt a proactive approach that offers them the most flexibility in the face of changing and unpredictable conditions.

## Conservation Actions Classification SF5

1.1 Site/Area Stewardship In oak-dominated natural communities, maintain or increase diversity of oak species as appropriate for site conditions through various silvicultural techniques such as, planting, etc., to improve resilience to pests, disease and environmental change. At the site level, employ an eight-part approach to non-native invasive species: 1) careful planning; 2) prevention; 3) early detection and rapid response; 4) control; 5) slowing the spread; 6) reducing impacts; 7) monitoring; 8) restoration.

System Management & Modifications -Fire & Fire Management]

[FIGURE: invasive plants, earthworms, and overabundant native plants and animals present serious challenges to the persistence of southern forests. Invasive plants are prolific reproducers in the absence of their homeland's natural checks and balances and can outcompete native plants by monopolizing light, water or nutrient resources. The most common non-native invasive plants of southern forests include herbs such as garlic mustard (Alliaria petiolata) and shrubs such as common buckthorn (Rhamnus cathartica) and Eurasian bush honeysuckles (Lonicera spp.). Non-native invasive earthworms disrupt normal nutrient cycling by rapidly consuming all of the leaf litter, thus exposing the soil to erosion, compaction, and invasion by non-native invasive plants. Local white-tailed deer populations can limit regeneration and growth of trees and ground layer plants. Climate change is likely to exacerbate the impacts of invasive exotic and overabundant native plants and animals.]

*Issue SF4-Habitat Fragmentation from Development, Conversion, Systems Modification .1 Site/Area Stewardship-Invasive/problematic species control -Inventory and early detection 8.2 Evaluation, Effectiveness Measures & Learning Conduct regular monitoring of sites to detect new invasions and to evaluate the success of pest management plans and control measures. Enhance structural complexity of forests by retaining and promoting features such as large cavity trees, snags, and course woody debris. Promote uneven aged stand management where appropriate. In harvest planning mimic natural disturbance patterns whenever possible, such as feathering edges of harvests, grouping leave trees in irregular shapes, patch or group harvest, and "Individuals, Clumps, Groups" harvest approaches tailored to regionally specific metrics (ICO, Churchill et al. 2016),( Razenkova et al. 2025) 6 . Work toward a balanced mosaic of tree age classes by developing clear acreage goals and geographic priority areas for both young forest and old forest. Consider phasing stand ages through time, allowing a stand to convert from young forest through old forest instead of attempting to maintain a stand in a stage in perpetuity, such as maintaining young forest via frequent harvests. Practice Green Tree Retention during forest management to promote species, structural and size class diversity within stands. Leave groups with interlocked crowns instead of individuals to promote wind fastness and simulate natural disturbance patterns. Natural System Management & Modifications Many forest-dwelling plants and animals depend on large blocks of uninterrupted forested habitat. Habitat fragmentation, either through conversion to developed or other non-forest land, or converting one type of forest to another, such as natural forest to pine plantation, reduces habitat for species needing large blocks of mature forest, such as forest interior birds.*

## Elements 3 & 4. Threats/Issues and Conservation Actions Wetland Community Group

This is a summary of threats/issues and conservation actions that are common to all or most of the community types 1 in the wetland community group. As much as possible, the source of the threat is described as well as the stresses or effects that occur directly or indirectly as a result of the threat. Stresses are generally thought of as loss, conversion and/or degradation of the natural community. Distinguishing the source of the impact from the effects that occur to or in the community is important because the two typically need a different approach and set of conservation actions. Multiple sources of impact may have the same or similar effects on a community. Similar effects may be addressed collectively by a single action or suite of actions.
The wetland group includes the following community types 2 :
• Alder Thicket • American Lotus-lily Marsh • Bog Relict • Boreal Rich Fen • Calcareous Fen • Central Poor Fen • Coastal Plain Marsh • Emergent Marsh • Ephemeral Pond
• Floating-leaved Marsh • Great Lakes Shore Fen (see also Great Lakes group) • Great Lakes Ridge and Swale (see also Great Lakes group)
• Interdunal Wetland • Moist Sandy Meadow • Muskeg • Northern Sedge Meadow • Oligotrophic Marsh • Open Bog • Patterned Peatland • Poor Fen • Shrub-carr • Southern Sedge Meadow • Submergent Marsh • Wild Rice Marsh
1 Community or natural community is used in the WWAP as a proxy for habitat.
2 For descriptions of the community types, visit 
https://dnr.wi.gov
, search "biodiversity".
Conservation actions for most or all wetland community types are organized according to categories in the Conservation Actions Classification based on the Open Standards threats and actions classification
foot_0
 . If the threat/issue and its associated conservation action(s) apply to one or a few species they are identified as such. Conservation actions overlapping in content or scope may be grouped under a single code.
More about how threats and issues or conservation actions were developed and how this information can be used to make conservation decisions can be found in E3-4 Threats & Actions of the 2025 WWAP collection. Wisconsin has lost 47% of its original ten million acres of wetlands since Euro-American settlement. Many of the remaining 5.3 million acres are in the northern third of the state 
(Wisconsin DNR 1990)
. In some southern Wisconsin counties, the amount of wetland loss is well over 75%. Wisconsin's losses are reflective of the national status of wetlands; it is estimated that one-half of the nation's original 221 million acres of wetlands have been lost 
(Feierabend 1992)
. A large amount of remaining acreage in Wisconsin exists in a partly altered state, such as with old drainage ditches still functional enough to change the hydrology of the wetland. Much of this remaining wetland acreage was at one time disturbed, either by drainage (followed by restoration) or by being cleared, repeatedly burned, grazed, or periodically plowed Elements 3 & 4 Threats and Actions -Wetland Community Group 9/8/2025 
[Draft]
 Wisconsin Department of Natural Resources Page 3 of 9 
(Curtis 1959)
. Loss of wetlands adversely affects a wide range of wetland functions, from wildlife habitat to water quality to flood storage.

## Most commonly cited threats included in this report:

## Conservation Actions Classification We1

Depending on your overall objectives, the following conservation actions can address habitat fragmentation and the effects that it has on non-forested wetland natural communities: Maintain and restore native plant communities within the 100 year floodplain along rivers and streams.
6.4
Target wetland restoration, enhancement, and preservation of priority areas identified through Wisconsin Watershed Approach to Wetland Functional Assessment. Excess nutrients, usually in the form of nitrogen and/or phosphorus, favor non-native invasive species (e.g., reed canary grass) and aggressive native species (e.g., cat-tails) which displace native plants. Nutrients can come from a variety of sources, ranging from agricultural fields to lawns. In addition, nitrogen accumulates through atmospheric deposition, mainly due to the burning of fossil fuels. Sedimentation is also problematic, and can arise from unsustainable agricultural practices on steep slopes or near waterways, land-clearing activities, unsustainable timber harvest operation, and poorly designed road crossings at streams or wetlands. Overall, sedimentation increases water turbidity and can cover low-lying plants in silt. Following water quality BMPs greatly reduces the risk from these activities; however, climate change may add complexity to this issue as severe precipitation events are projected to increase and the season of frozen ground conditions grows shorter.

## Threat/Issue We2-Hydrologic Alteration

## Conservation Actions Classification We3

Depending on your overall objectives, the following conservation actions can be considered to address nutrient enrichment and sedimentation and the effects that they can have on wetland communities: 

## Law Enforcement

## Conservation Actions Classification We4

Depending on your overall objectives, the following conservation actions can address invasive species and the effects that they have on wetland natural communities:
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship Maintain open character and limit invasive species and brush invasion in open wetlands and sedge meadows through the use of herbicide, prescribed fire, and other techniques. Prevent and minimize the spread of invasive species by following best management practices
foot_2
 . The 2018 Wisconsin Aquatic Invasive Species Management Plan
foot_3
 , which provides additional goals and actions to prevent, contain and control aquatic invasive species. Prior to Euro-American settlement, some open wetland types experienced regular fire, especially communities such as Calcareous Fen, Southern Sedge Meadow, and Coastal Plain Marsh that occur within fire-dependent landscapes. Fire had the effect of setting back woody species, stimulating grasses, sedges, and wildflowers (especially smaller seeded species and annuals), and volatizing excess nitrogen. However, since the early part of the 20 th century, fires have been actively suppressed, leading to ecological simplification through shrub encroachment and increased dominance by tall, coarse perennials.

## Conservation Actions Classification We5

Depending on your overall objectives, the following conservation actions can be considered to address fire suppression and the effects that it has on wetland natural communities:
1 Anticipated hydrologic impacts include increases in extreme precipitation events as well as longer dry periods in between rain events. Large precipitation or snow melt events also increase the risk of invasive species being spread to new areas, increase erosion and sedimentation, and increase nutrient runoff, which further fuels the growth of non-native invasive plants. The impact of these events is likely to be greatest lower in the watershed, where flood waters collect for a longer period of time (Zedler 2009). In addition, increasingly variable winter conditions may impact groundwater infiltration vs. surface runoff as well.
Overall, climate change is likely to interact with other stressors such as invasive species, land use changes, and anthropogenic water use to pose a high degree of risk to many wetland communities, with select communities being less vulnerable.

## Conservation Actions Classification We6

Depending on your overall objectives, the following conservation actions can be considered to encourage climate change adaptation for wetland natural communities:
1 Increase groundwater infiltration practices and decrease stormwater input and nutrient enrichment of water from impervious surfaces (parking lots, etc.) through techniques such as the installation of bioswales, rain gardens, etc.

## Alliance & Partnership Development

Work with agricultural stakeholders to balance water quality and water quantity with planting design, crop selection, discontinuous vegetative cover, tillage practices, nutrient management, pest management, and irrigation.

[TABLE]
* Invasive species displace native plants and associated animals, lower species diversity, alter structural diversity (such as the ratio of open water pools to vegetation, abundance and distribution of sedge tussocks, etc.) and alter nutrient cycling. The most problematic non-native invasive species in open wetlands include well-established plants such as reed canary grass, narrow-leaved and hybrid cat-tail, purple loosestrife, and non-native Phragmites, as well as more recent, rapidly spreading invaders like Japanese hops. Invasive species are often exacerbated by soil disturbance, sedimentation, and nutrient enrichment. Invasive species are expected to increase over time due to both natural spread as well as climate change, as they are able to take advance of longer growing seasons and rapidly respond to disturbances such as large storm events.*

| Non-forested wetland communities are projected to range from low to high vulnerability to |
| climate change, with vulnerability highly dependent on the type of wetland (Climate Change |
| Vulnerability Assessment Workshops 2014). In general, potential changes to hydrology are |
| projected to have the greatest impact on wetlands with narrow hydrologic parameters, |
| especially temperature and water level (e.g., fens). Communities adapted to a wider variety of |
| water levels (e.g, sedge meadows) or with the capacity to buffer against small changes |
| (northern peatlands) are projected to have moderate vulnerability, whereas community a high |
| tolerance to variable hydrology and nutrients (e.g., shrub-carr and emergent marsh) are |
| projected to have the lowest vulnerability. |

[/TABLE]

Protecting or conserving SEFs does not translate to protecting specific SGCNs, but rather biodiversity in general, recognizing that the distribution and abundance of species and their habitat is changing.
Each SEF is assigned to a geographic scale of uniqueness based on common expert knowledge: global, continental, Midwest-regional or state. These categories can assist users to communicate priorities in different contexts. Place examples are provided for each SEF.

## SEF 1. Bedrock Communities Geographic scale of uniqueness: State

Bedrock communities can be found throughout the state in a variety of forms and landscape settings, and often support unique and extreme microclimates. Some natural communities that are present with these features are extremely rare, including Alvar (also treated under SEF 8. Great Lakes and their Shorelines), Algific Talus Slope, Bedrock Glade, Glaciere Talus, and Talus Forest. Wisconsin has two cliff communities included in this feature that, while not rare, support many rare or declining plant and animal species (e.g., timber rattlesnake on Dry Cliffs and federally listed northern monkshood on Moist Cliffs). Priority opportunities exist where bedrock communities occur within a matrix of supportive natural cover types such that essential microclimates are preserved (e.g., sufficient canopy cover growing above and around Moist Cliff, Algific Talus Slope and Glaciere Talus). Sites with diverse microhabitats conferred by a variety of slopes, aspects, and microclimates should also rank higher in conservation assessments as they allow species to shift through space and time as environmental conditions change. The Niagara Escarpment is a bedrock feature that is highlighted as a separate Significant Ecological Feature given its very broad extent and influence on the regional landscape.

## Place Examples by Ecological Landscape 1

Central Sand Plains -In the Central Sand Plains the influence of Glacial Lake Wisconsin -especially where its outflow breached the Dells of the Wisconsin River --resulted in a concentration of cliff communities. Often the most visible portions of this generally flat landscape, these buttes, mesas and other cliff communities provide limited habitat for SGCN and need to be managed as small patches where they are located. Natural Communities: Dry Cliff, Moist Cliff. Example: Mill Bluff State Park.
Forest Transition -Bedrock exposures are uncommon in this ecological landscape but provide significant habitat for unique species where they occur. Most of these are associated with large rivers, including the St. Croix River near St. Croix Falls, the Eau Claire River, and the Wolf River as it crosses the Menominee Indian Reservation and parts of Langlade County. There are also important quartzite glades, talus slopes, and glaciere talus at Rib Mountain in Marathon County. Natural communities: Moist Cliff, Dry Cliff, Bedrock Glade, Glaciere Talus, Talus Forest. Northeast Sands -In the Northeast Sands, the bedrock was so resistant to the effects of glacial grind and wear that many bedrock outcrops persist, supporting Bedrock Glades 2 embedded in Northern Dry and Dry-mesic Forest. These Bedrock Glades often contain plants and animals that are different from the surrounding forest and need to be managed as small patches where they are located. Examples:
• Hagar Mountain SNA and Sunrise Lake SNA (within Nicolet Dry Forest COA (15.03) • Grand Island SNA (not within a COA).
Western Prairie -Dry and Moist Cliff communities occur in association with riparian corridors, Dry Prairie and Oak Opening. Examples:
• Within Lower St. Croix Uplands COA (6.01): Apple River Canyon SNA.
• Within Kinnickinnic River Gorge COA (6.03): Kinnickinnic River Gorge and Delta SNA.
North Central Forest -The Blue Hills area contains unique geological features especially the Felsenmeers ("sea of rocks"), which consist of extensive slopes of open, shattered quartzite talus with unusual lichen communities and dramatic cold air drainages which are responsible for the presence of several notably disjunct northern species. Examples: 

## SEF 3. Caves, Abandoned Mines and Tunnels

Geographic scale of uniqueness: Midwest Regional
Wisconsin has approximately 150 natural caves, abandoned mines, and former rail tunnels that have become hibernacula and mating sites for Wisconsin's four hibernating bat species, all of which have been impacted by White-nose Syndrome. As of 2025, more than 40% of the U.S. population of little brown bats--nearly 100,000 individuals --overwinter in only three of these sites: Maiden Rock Mine and Bay City Mine along the Mississippi River, and Neda Mine on the Niagara Escarpment 
(WI DNR Bat Program, unpublished data, 2025)
.
A few smaller hibernation sites in the Penokee Range, Door Peninsula, and Central Sands remain important for significant numbers of little brown bats and tricolored bats. Certain hibernacula, including former rail tunnels and natural caves in the Driftless Area and Central Sands region serve large numbers of big brown bats along with other species. Despite severe population declines from White-nose Syndrome, Northern long-eared bats are still found using several hibernacula around Wisconsin each winter, though site choice by these individuals is difficult to predict.

## Place Examples by Ecological Landscape

Central Sand Hills -Cold streams and streamside wet-mesic prairie and calcareous fen communities. Example: as an important site for grassland birds. Other taxa groups may use the grasslands too, including rare species of small mammals, turtles, and invertebrates such as butterflies, moths, and bumble bees. The recreation area is located within the Bong Grasslands COA (12.02).
• COA Chaffee Creek (A.04)
Western Prairie -Though original unplowed prairie remnants are few and small in this EL, extensive acreages of surrogate grassland habitat are protected within the Western Prairie Habitat Restoration Area and scattered federal Waterfowl Protection Areas, providing priority 'prairie pothole' habitat for grassland birds, small mammals and lepidopterans (butterflies and moths). Some but not all the sites identified below fall within the Prairie Potholes COA (6.02).
Examples:
• Cylon Wildlife Area and SNA 

## SEF 7. Glacial Lake Wisconsin

Geographic scale of uniqueness: Midwest Regional
The bed of Glacial Lake Wisconsin occurs in central Wisconsin and is a biodiversity hotspot. Barrens and pine-oak forests occur on level to rolling uplands and are juxtaposed with large wetland complexes, both of which are occasionally punctuated by towering sandstone buttes, ridges, and pinnacles. The habitat diversity combined with the location along the climatic Tension Zone and supports a unique mixture of southern and northern species. Many SGCN, especially habitat and area-sensitive species, thrive in the area. Due in part to sandy soils unsuited for row crops, large blocks of public land are found here. This large expanse of public land provides some of the best opportunities to manage for the full continuum of pine-oak forest to barrens to wetlands in the upper Midwest. 

## SEF 8. Great Lakes and their Shorelines Geographic scale of uniqueness: Global

The Great Lakes are the largest freshwater lakes in the world. Their shorelines and nearshore waters provide hundreds of miles of mostly continuous habitat for hundreds of species. Known for supporting endemic plants and animals like dwarf lake iris and Piping Plover, the Great Lakes are also part of the one of the major migratory flyways of North America and are utilized by millions of birds each year. Sandspits and coastal barrier spits such as Long Island and Wisconsin Point, as well as the restored Long Tail Point in Green Bay are especially important and support large concentrations of migratory birds while tiny islands provide habitat for nesting colonial waterbirds. The sandscapes of Lakes Superior and Michigan are also unique with beaches and dunes providing critical habitat for rare plants and invertebrates.
Islands in the Great Lakes provide unique habitat. The Apostle Islands have exceptional examples of old growth forests, beach and dune complexes, coastal wetlands, and bedrock features that support a tremendous regional repository of rare biota. The Door Peninsula and Grand Traverse Islands that stretch northeastward have high concentrations of rare species associated with the calcareous soils and exposures of dolomite that characterize shorelines.
The freshwater estuaries on the south shore of Lake Superior in Wisconsin are among the highest quality in the entire Great Lakes region, having better water quality, more intact natural communities, fewer invasive species, and less development in the surrounding landscape than most sites along Lakes Michigan, Huron, and Erie. Several Great Lakes ridge and swale complexes can also be found in Wisconsin, though they are more abundant in Michigan.
Containing alternating series of parallel wetlands and forested upland ridges, they support incredible diversity in a small geographic area and provide important habitat for a variety of species, including plants, herptiles, and nesting and migrating birds.

## Place Examples by Ecological Landscape

Central Lake Michigan Coastal -In this EL, the Lake Michigan shoreline harbors most of the features described above, from sandscapes to coastal wetlands to alvar. One large-scale restoration project is the Cat Island Chain, a cluster of islands that protect more than 1,300 acres of shallow embayments and coastal wetlands, providing nesting, resting, and foraging habitat for resident and migratory waterbirds. Examples of sites that highlight different features:
Great Lakes sandscapes Southern Lake Michigan Coastal -Beach and dune communities are less common here than along the central and northern portion of the Lake Michigan shoreline. While many that remain are highly impacted by recreational users, seawalls, invasive species, and development, some are still protected and offer important ecosystem services. The lake and nearshore area provide critical habitat for wintering waterfowl, while nearshore areas with natural habitats provide safe roosts, food and water for migrating landbirds and, in the fall, migrating raptors. The SLMC contains Chiwaukee Prairie, one of the Upper Midwest's premier lakeplain prairie complexes. Subtle variations in topography created by the progressive recession of Glacial Lake Nipissing resulted in a complex mosaic of rare natural communities at this site such as wet prairie, wetmesic prairie, calcareous fen, southern sedge meadow, and emergent marsh. Apart from Chiwaukee Prairie SNA (within the Chiwaukee Prairie COA [12.01]), other important sites in the EL are Migratory Bird Concentration Sites: Geographic scale of uniqueness: Midwest Regional Large blocks of northern forests in Wisconsin provide essential habitats for a wide range of wildlife, including many species that depend on large, contiguous forest areas for survival from pine martens to wolves to breeding birds. Forming part of the core of the northern hardwoods of the Great Lakes region, these forests are centers of abundance for many broadly distributed species such as northern warbler species as well as uncommon species such as northern goshawk. Old forests are particularly important for species that need complex structure such as supercanopy trees, large snags, and abundance downed woody debris. Northern hardwood forests include embedded lakes, wetlands, bedrock and particularly in the northeast present exposed rock outcrops (cliffs, talus slopes and glades) that support Northern Mesic Forest, Northern Dry-Mesic Forest, Northern Wet-Mesic Forest, Northern Wet Forest, Open Bog, Muskeg, Northern Hardwood Swamp, Northern Sedge Meadow, and Bedrock Glades.
Large blocks of older forests are important not only for forested uplands but also for the variety of other habitats large landscapes include such as cedar swamps and undeveloped lakes of the Winegar Moraine in northern Vilas and Iron County and the Nicolet Hemlock Hardwoods area in Forest and Florence Co. to high-gradient streams, forested watersheds, and rock outcrops of the Penokee Hills in Ashland and Iron County to the Flambeau River State Forest peppered with ephemeral ponds and its namesake river in Sawyer County. These minimally fragmented complexes of high-quality habitat in proximity provide exceptional benefit to wildlife. Large blocks are also more resistant to catastrophic windthrow than small, isolated stands. In most areas, older forests are embedded in a relatively intact matrix of forest managed for timber, providing additional habitat of mixed successional stages and age classes. The broad areas of relatively continuous forested habitat also serve as a regionally important climate migration pathway.
The Menominee Reservation has vast relatively unbroken hemlock-hardwood forests, scattered lakes, and ecologically important streams within forested watersheds. Large white cedar swamps are common in the eastern portion, where marl lakes supporting calciphilic plants occur. Prominent exposures of granitic bedrock occur along the Wolf River. Most of the forest is older than average for the state and supports significant populations of forest interior species that have become scarce in forests elsewhere. The Upper Mississippi serves as a vital habitat for diverse wildlife, including fish, migratory birds, and other aquatic organisms, supporting rich ecosystems along the northern portion of the largest river in North America. The Lower Wisconsin is the longest free-flowing stretches of river in Midwest and supports both plants, fish, and aquatic invertebrates that depend on its mostly natural dam-free hydrologic regime. The Lower Wolf River is one of the few remaining rivers with a high degree of natural meandering, required by some aquatic species including rare mussels. The St. Croix River is also home to a disproportionally large number of mussel species, including several that are endangered. These river systems also support large floodplain forests and associated riverine lakes and sloughs, which provide highly connected habitat for migratory and breeding birds, herps, and plants. As corridors with abundant natural habitat, they are also important for climate migration and larger landscape-level resiliency. Due to geologic history and geomorphology, all floodplain complexes associated with large rivers also occur in the Driftless Region and could be treated under either category depending on whether viewed from a terrestrial or aquatic conservation perspective.

## Place Examples by

opportunities feature large areas with diverse wetland types, microtopographical variation, and intact hydrology that allow for species and associations to shift through time and space in response to changing environmental conditions. Bonus points can be added for those sites with supportive natural habitats in the surrounding uplands. Also see "Grassland Landscapes" for large expanses of wetlands that may have lower ecological integrity but that provide vital habitat for grassland birds.

## SEF 13. Medium-sized Rivers and Streams

Geographic scale of uniqueness: Midwest Regional Wisconsin supports numerous medium-sized rivers of Midwest significance. Medium-sized rivers and streams are moderate to large but are still wadeable. In the Wisconsin DNR riverine natural classification, they are classified as cool, cold and warm mainstem streams with annual 90% exceedance flow ranges from 1.0 -110 ft 3 /s. River systems such as the Upper Wolf River, Namekagon, Lemonweir, Bark and Pine-Popple fall into this category. Some of these rivers support some of the best populations of globally imperiled wood turtles in the Great Lakes region. These riparian areas also contain significant populations of fish and rare invertebrates such as mussels and dragonflies, though often have fewer species than their larger counterparts. Medium-sized rivers also serve as major migratory bird stopover areas and often abundance of white-tailed deer has left structural relicts of savannas with scattered opengrown oaks and decimated ground layers. The Southern Unit of the Kettle Moraine State Forest, portions of the Central Sand Hills and Central Sand Plains Ecological Landscapes, and some places in the Western Coulees and Ridges Ecological Landscape, are areas where significant management opportunities exist for these communities. Bedrock Glades are also included here because they support similar structure, characteristic species, associated SGCN, and threats; Wisconsin has the best conservation opportunities in the world for conservation of certain types of this community such as quartzite bedrock glades of the Baraboo Hills within the Western Coulee and Ridges.

Boreal Transition Forest Geographic scale of uniqueness: Midwest Regional Boreal Transition Forest occurs in far northern Wisconsin and the northern part of the Door Peninsula where cooler temperatures and local climate conditions allow northern species more typically associated with Canada to survive. This includes Boreal Forest on dry uplands as well as Black Spruce Swamp that occurs as part of a broader mosaic of peat-dominated wetlands such as open bog, muskeg and tamarack swamp. Boreal Transition Forest provides important habitat for boreal birds in Wisconsin as well as several rare plants that prefer cooler temperatures. | The Driftless Area is a vital region for conservation due to its unique glacial history, geological |
| --- |
| features and rich biodiversity. Unlike surrounding landscapes smoothed by glacial activity, the |
| Driftless Area was untouched by the last glacial period and boasts steep hills, bedrock ridges, |
| and deep valleys carved by streams and rivers that support diverse plant and animal species, |
| some of which are rare or endemic. High landscape heterogeneity, including forests, wetlands, |
| and bluff prairies have resulted in ecological richness that is crucial for SGCN habitat. The area |
| plays a significant role in protecting water quality in local rivers and streams, making it essential |
| for ecosystem health. Approximately 75% of the Driftless Area is found in southwest Wisconsin, |
| with the remaining occurring in southeast Minnesota, northeast Iowa, and a small part of |
| northwest Illinois. |
| The rugged topography influences land use patterns and has resulted in a more intact natural |
| landscape compared to glaciated areas that were more easily developed or converted to |
| extensive agriculture. Natural community types and habitats that are especially important here |
| are southern dry-mesic forests, southern mesic forests, floodplain forests, hemlock and pine |

*Kettle Moraine Features Geographic scale of uniqueness: Midwest Regional The This EL has it all, from sandscapes to rockscapes, coastal wetlands to cliffs, boreal forest to vast peatlands, migratory and winter bird concentration areas. One unique feature is captured by the Bad River COA (1.02) on land owned and managed by the Bad River Band of Lake Superior Chippewa. One of the highest quality and most extensive coastal wetlands in the Great Lakes is found where the Kakagon and Bad Rivers flow into Lake Superior in Ashland County. At more than 16,000 acres, this vast wetland complex offers vital spawning areas for native fishes as well as important migratory stopover habitat for tens of thousands of passerines, raptors, shorebirds, and waterbirds. The Kakagon-Bad River Sloughs complex was designated a Wetland of International Importance under the Ramsar Convention on Wetlands in 2012. High quality dune communities and boreal forest are also on Bad River tribal lands.Apostle Islands National Lake Shore (owned and managed by National Park Service) harbors one of the largest island complexes on a freshwater lake in the world. The sandscapes, cliffs, forests and barrier beach lagoons are some of the least disturbed and most diverse ecosystems on the western Great Lakes. Example SNAs (all within the Apostle Islands COA [1.03]): Kettle Moraine is a large glacial interlobate moraine starting east of Lake Winnebago and running southwest for almost 90 miles into southeastern Wisconsin. The feature is contained within the Southeast Glacial Plains Ecological Landscape and features rugged topography and contains many glacial features such as kames, drumlins and eskers. The vegetation is a complex of uplands, wetlands, and rivers including Northern Wet Forest, Southern Dry Forest, Southern Dry-mesic Forest, Southern Mesic Forest, Oak Woodland, Shrub Carr, Alder Thicket, Calcareous Fen, Bog Relict, Southern Sedge Meadow, Dry Prairie, Emergent Marsh, Submergent Aquatic. Presently it is a large, forested block embedded in an agricultural landscape. Michigan has some similar topography but the interlobate moraine in Wisconsin was less suitable for conversion to agriculture than other regions and many of the natural features that have persisted here have all but disappeared elsewhere. Interlobate moraines with this combination of natural features at this scale are very rare, and possibly restricted to just a few locations in the Upper Midwest.*

[Mississippi River (A.10)
 harbor significant streamside natural communities. While these riparian corridors are often smaller than those along large rivers, they still support a tremendous diversity of forested and non-forested wetlands in proximity ranging from floodplain forests to shrub-carr to emergent marsh. Some marshes also support wild rice, though in lower densities and abundance than occur on northern lakes. The concentration of natural habitats along these riparian corridors also make medium-sized rivers important for climate migration, and in more agricultural parts of the state form some of the only remaining natural habitat for a wide variety of wildlife. The sites with the highest conservation significance are those that support a diverse aquatic community of fishes, herps, and invertebrates along with high-quality and diverse forested and non-forested wetlands. 

## SEF 15. Niagara Escarpment

## Geographic scale of uniqueness: Global

The Niagara Escarpment is a bedrock feature composed mostly of Silurian dolomite that arcs from Lake Champlain in the northeastern United States westward across the Bruce Peninsula of Ontario through the Upper Peninsula of Michigan and southward through eastern Wisconsin.
Its length and influence on the landscape warrant highlighting it as a separate feature, apart from Bedrock Communities. Here the Escarpment is exposed from the islands off the northern tip of the Door Peninsula southwest for over 150 miles into southeastern Wisconsin before it is buried beneath glacial deposits. The Escarpment supports many rare species, including providing habitat for globally rare snails, and hibernacula for bats in addition to being an important north-south migratory corridor with stopover habitat for migratory birds and bats.
Cliffs along the Escarpment also support the oldest trees known in Wisconsin with some individuals over 1,000 years old. Important natural communities associated with the Escarpment include globally rare alvar as well as moist cliffs, dry cliffs, caves, talus forests, along with a type of oak woodland that borders on limestone bedrock glade specific to the Niagara Escarpment.
Northern Lake Michigan Coastal -Examples:
• Peninsula Niagara Escarpment SNA (within Baileys Harbor to Peninsula COA [14.12]) 

## SEF 16. Northern Highland Kettle Lakes and Pine Forest

Geographic scale of uniqueness: Midwest Regional
The Northern Highland region boasts one of the highest densities of glacial kettle lakes in North America surrounded by extensive red and white pine forest and high-quality wetlands. While lakes are common in many glaciated areas across the northern U.S. and Canada, most concentrations occur on bedrock driven landscapes, such as the Canadian shield. The sandy pitted outwash of the Northern Highland region supports some of the region's only oligotrophic seepage lakes, whose circumneutral waters, exceptionally low nutrients, and firm bottoms support a unique aquatic plant flora and rare invertebrates. Other lakes and low-gradient streams support lush beds of wild rice, which are important both ecologically and culturally. While many lakes in the region have been developed or hydrologically modified, a significant number of wild lakes still occur here, and these are the focus of conservation efforts.
This region presents an opportunity to conserve and manage large-scale white pine and red pine forests, which thrive in the cool climate and nutrient-poor sandy soils. Wildlife species associated with coniferous forests are especially well-represented here. Some of the most unique pine forests occur adjacent to lake margins and large peatland complexes, where periodic surface fires and drying winds have maintained a more open and heterogeneous structure than is typically found in fire-suppressed stands. Pine regeneration is currently lacking in many sites due to overly dense and shady subcanopies, but prescribed fire as well as silvicultural techniques can be used to manage and restore these forests along with the species that depend on them. Northern mesic forests dominated by hemlock and northern hardwoods also occur here and are an important part of the extensive forest cover that provides diverse wildlife habitat and opportunities for species migration related to climate change.
Northern Highland -Historically, the Northern Highlands were the site of Wisconsin's greatest pinery, which are recovering in some areas with large trees and natural regeneration. The Northern Highland-American Legion State Forest offers Wisconsin's best opportunity to protect, manage, and restore forests of large white and red pines. Site capability, historical events, scattered remnants, current patterns of succession, and a large public land base make this possible at multiple scales. Forest restoration priorities have been identified here for eastern white pine, red pine, eastern hemlock, tamarack, and jack pine. The Northern Highland EL also boasts a density of lakes rivaled in few other parts of the world, making this EL globally important for this resource. Seepage lakes are the most abundant type here, with soft and very soft types particularly unique and important for conservation initiatives. Examples: Northern Lake Michigan Coastal -In this EL, concentrations of wetlands occur along the west shore of Green Bay, in estuaries and embayments near Lake Michigan, and in the area north and east of Lake Noquebay. On the Door Peninsula, important peatlands are associated with the groundwater-fed basins of the major coastal embayments, and the lowlands occupying the ridge-and-swale landforms along Lake Michigan. Examples:
• Lake Noquebay Sedge Meadow (within Lake Noquebay COA [14.21]) Superior Coastal Plain -While Bibon Swamp is an interior wetland, most large peatlands occur along the Lake Superior coastline. In fact, the southwestern portion of the Lake Superior basin contains more high-quality coastal wetlands than any other region in the Great Lakes. Many of these are associated with drowned river mouths or barrier beach lagoons and are considered freshwater estuaries that host a complex and distinctive mosaic of natural communities. Examples:
• Big Bay Sand Spit and Bog SNA, (within the Lake Superior COA [A.01]) Midwest Oak and Pine Barrens are globally rare types of savanna that support a disproportionate number of rare species relative to other natural communities, including federally endangered species such as Karner blue butterfly (Lycaeides melissa samuelis) and Kirtland's warbler (Setophaga kirtlandii). Barrens restoration and management was identified as a high priority by the Wisconsin DNR in 2017. Most barrens have been degraded through past grazing, severely altered fire regimes, invasive species, and fragmentation. Wisconsin has some of the best opportunities across the North American range of Pine Barrens to protect, manage, and maintain this globally rare natural community. Pine and Oak Barrens in Wisconsin occur within dynamic fire-driven ecosystems and can be managed as part of a continuum from semiopen brush prairie to savanna with scattered trees to closed canopy dry forest. Small remnants exhibiting high floristic diversity are important to identify, protect and manage, with surrounding barrens of lower quality providing buffers and opportunities for restoration and expansion. To accommodate area-sensitive animals, coordination between diverse landowners and property managers to manage for large barrens landscapes is essential. 

## SEF 20. Southeast Glacial Plain Marshes

## Geographic scale of uniqueness: State

An aggregation of deep marshes and hemimarshes in the Southeast Glacial Plains EL supports significant resident wildlife populations (particularly waterfowl, marsh birds, and colonial waterbirds) and hosts major concentrations of migratory birds in spring and fall. While many may not qualify as high-quality wetlands from a floristic standpoint, they offer essential habitat including food, cover and shelter for numerous SGCN. These extensive deep marshes and hemimarshes provide an opportunity to manage a broad range of SGCN in concert with other programs focused on waterfowl. These communities can be managed to maximize SGCN diversity in addition to waterfowl targets. Adjacent natural communities can be managed to buffer core areas from the surrounding croplands that dominate this landscape, as well as to expand functional habitat for area sensitive species and meet the needs of species that migrate between uplands and wetlands throughout their life history (e.g., some herptiles).

## Place Examples by Ecological Landscape

Southeast Glacial Plain: Examples:
• Horicon Marsh Wildlife Area and National Wildlife Refuge (within Horicon Marsh COA [9.09]) • Rush Lake SNA (within Rush Lake COA [9.07])

Wisconsin's approach to public involvement has been to engage people and organiza�ons for development of each itera�on of the WWAP. It includes federal and state agency representa�ves, DNR staff, stakeholders, landowners, tribal na�ons, individuals and organiza�ons that have an interest in the goals of the WWAP.
